## I / 一

===Original===
On King Olaf’s Howe

Eric the Victorious was in that time King in Upsala, the son of Biorn the Old, the son of Eric, the son of Emund, and had dominion over the Up-Swedes and over the folk of Nether-realm and Southmanland and East and West Gautland and over all countries and kingdoms eastaway from the Elf to the main sea. King Eric was as now in his old years, and was held for a man of mickle might and worship, sitting in that state and stead whereas his forefathers aforetime sat from the days of Ragnar Hairy-breeks; and in their veins was the blood of the kin of the Ynglings, even from that far time when the Gods came first from Asgarth of ancient days, and Yngvi-Frey dwelt among mankind in kingdom in Upsala.

Now when King Biorn the Old was come to die, he left his two sons Eric and Olaf joint Kings in Sweden, and forty years they reigned together in good brotherly love and friendship. King Olaf Biornson had to wife Ingibiorg, daughter of Earl Thrand of Sula. Their children were Thora and Thurid and Asdis and Aud. Ungainly it seemed to King Olaf that he should get none but girl-children. Howso, in the end it befell that Queen Ingibiorg was brought to bed of a man-child, and he was sprinkled with water and named Biorn after his father’s father. But the Queen lived not long, but died the third year after this; and when the lad was but five winters old, then was King Olaf his father fallen down dead of a sudden, where he sat at ale-drinking in his hall. And it was the talk of men that there was venom in the cup, and that that was the bane of King Olaf. So he was laid in howe at Upsala. Thereafter was Eric taken for sole King in Sweden, and he brought up the lad and put him to fostering with Earl Wolf, his mother’s brother. No children of King Eric born in wedlock were as then alive, and his wife now ten years dead. Dearly he loved his brother’s son, and tendered him as he had been his own bairn. And the lad waxed up the goodliest to look on and strongest and likeliest of lads, tall and great-sinewed beyond his years. And because the lad was somewhat grim and stubborn of will and hasty and sudden of anger and very fierce and proud, even now in his tender youth, King Eric let lengthen his name and let call him Styrbiorn.

Against the coming of winter, when Styrbiorn was now fifteen winters old, Eric the King made great blood-offering in the temple at Upsala for the goodness of the year, as was his wont and the wont of his fathers before him from time immemorial. Thither were come together lords and great men of account from up and down the land, and there was great drinking toward. But Styrbiorn came not to meat that day, and came not to the King’s hall. So the King sent men to seek for Styrbiorn. In a while they returned, and a man of the King’s bodyguard said, “So it is, Lord, that we found him sitting on his father’s howe, King Olaf Biornson’s.”

The King’s brow darkened. He said to Earl Wolf, “Must this thing be, every autumn feast of sacrifice? Well, ’tis now the third time and the last; and yet it goeth nigh to anger me. Or will he not swallow my plighted word, that I will give him all, but not until he be sixteen year old?”

===Chinese===
奥拉夫王的坟冢上

常胜王埃里克其时在乌普萨拉为王；他是老比约恩之子，埃里克之孙，埃蒙德之曾孙，统有上瑞典诸部，兼领下国之地、南曼兰、东耶特兰与西耶特兰，以及自埃尔夫河迤逦向东直抵大洋的万邦列国。埃里克王如今年事已高，世人尊他为雄武而有威仪之主，端坐在那个座位、那片国土之上——自“毛裤”拉格纳的日子以来，他的列祖列宗便都坐在那里；他们的血脉里流着英格林王族的血，一直上溯到那遥远的年代：诸神初自古时的阿斯加德降临，英格维-弗雷居于人间，在乌普萨拉为王。

及至老比约恩王临终，他遗命二子埃里克与奥拉夫同为瑞典之王；兄弟二人共治四十年，友爱和睦，情谊甚笃。奥拉夫·比约恩森王娶妻英格比约格，为苏拉伯爵特朗德之女。他们的子女是托拉、图丽德、阿斯迪丝与奥德。奥拉夫王只得了这一群女孩，心下大为不悦。然而天从人愿，英格比约格王后末了果然产下一子；孩子行了洒水之礼，依他祖父之名，命名比约恩。只是王后寿不长久，此后第三年便故去了；孩子才五岁那年，他的父亲奥拉夫王正坐在厅中饮麦酒，忽然倒地暴亡。人们议论纷纷，说杯中下了毒，那便是奥拉夫王的死因。于是他被安葬在乌普萨拉的坟冢里。此后埃里克被立为瑞典唯一的王；他把这孩子抚养在身边，又托付给孩子的母舅沃尔夫伯爵教养。埃里克王婚生的子女彼时无一在世，王后也已死了十年。他深爱兄弟之子，抚之如亲生骨肉。这少年长成了最俊美、最健壮、最英挺的儿郎，身量高大，筋骨强健，远过同龄之人。只因这孩子性情有些阴沉执拗，易怒而暴烈，又极骄纵倨傲——纵在稚龄已然如此——埃里克王便为他把名字加长，唤作斯蒂尔比约恩。

将届冬时，斯蒂尔比约恩年已十五。埃里克王照例在乌普萨拉的神庙大行血祭，祈求年景丰足——这是他的惯例，也是自太古以来他列祖列宗的惯例。各地领主与有身份的贵人齐聚于此，大张饮宴。但斯蒂尔比约恩那日不来就席，也不到王的厅堂里来。王便遣人去寻他。过了一阵他们回来了，王的一名卫士说：“启禀主上，我们见他坐在他父亲的坟冢上，就是奥拉夫·比约恩森王的冢。”

王的双眉沉了下来。他对沃尔夫伯爵说：“每逢秋季祭宴，必出这般事么？罢了，这已是第三回，也是最后一回；可它险些要惹我动怒。难道他不肯咽下我许下的诺言——我自会把一切给他，只是非等他满十六岁不可？”

===Original===
“I looked not for this again, King,” said the Earl. “And truly I’m sorry for it.”

The King made them go again to bid him to the drinking. But back they came empty-handed. “Did he answer you naught?” asked the King.

At that, they were silent, looking one at the other. Then answered one for them all and said, “Naught, Lord, save this: that he would waste no breath on the King’s thralls.”

“Like as his father was,” said the King, “so is this young whelp. Go thou, Earl, if that might fetch him.”

Earl Wolf stood up and went betwixt the benches and the fires and forth of the main door into the King’s garth, and forth of the garth past the houses of men and the Thing-stead and the temple till he was come to the open field. It was murk and wild weather, and evening drawing in. Like a high house in bigness, Olaf’s howe reared against the fading light. It was all overgrown with rank grass, and the tufts and tussocks of the grass ducked and paled and rose and ducked again, slapped this way and that by the blustering squalls that charged and paused and swept again about the howe, ceaselessly as the ceaseless rush of vapours in the iron-gray windy lift of the sky overhead.

Styrbiorn sat on the top of the howe, unmoved as it, face to the wind. The Earl came up to him, fain to steady himself with a hand at whiles, what with the sudden squalls and the slippery grass. When he was up, “This is ill doing,” he said, shouting in his ear.

===Chinese===
“我原想不到又出了这等事，王，”伯爵说，“实在抱歉之至。”

王又差他们去请他来饮酒。他们却空手而回。“他没有答复你们什么吗？”王问。

众人闻言默然，面面相觑。随后一人替大家答道：“没有，主上，只除了一句：他说犯不着在王的奴仆身上白费口舌。”

“有其父必有其子，”王说，“这小狼崽子正是一路。你去吧，伯爵，兴许请得动他。”

沃尔夫伯爵站起身来，穿过条凳与火池之间，出了大门走进王的庭院，又出了庭院，经过人家的屋舍、议事场与神庙，一路来到旷野上。天色晦暝，风狂雨虐，暮色四合。奥拉夫王的坟冢在残照中耸起，大如一座高楼。冢上长满茂草，丛丛墩墩的草随风起伏，忽明忽暗，一起一伏，被阵阵呼啸的狂飙抽打得东倒西歪——那狂飙绕冢奔袭，忽而猛扑，忽而稍歇，忽而又横扫过来，永不止息，恰似头顶铁灰色高天狂风中云气奔流不息。

斯蒂尔比约恩坐在坟顶上，如冢本身一般纹丝不动，迎面向风。伯爵向他攀上去，狂风阵阵、青草湿滑，不时得腾出一只手来扶稳。爬到顶上，他凑着少年的耳朵喊道：“这是胡闹。”

===Original===
Styrbiorn moved not at all. He was muffled in a close-woven mantle of woollen stuff dyed purple and worked with black threads at the border in rich designs. Close as he held it about him, it puffed and flapped like a ship’s sail in a storm when the rudder is broken. His head was bare, and the thick short curled yellow hair on it leapt to the wind’s piping like the grass on the howe. He wore a heavy collar of pure gold, soft and bent to lie about his throat where neck and shoulder join, and worked by the goldsmith’s art with rich enchasements, and a dragon’s head at either end. He sat with his chin in his hands, frowning upwind so that his eyes watered.

The Earl sat down and put an arm about him. “The King withholdeth not thine inheritance, Styrbiorn. He hath promised, and he will give it thee, as well thou knowest. But time is not yet. Thou art yet but fifteen winters old.”

Styrbiorn shook him rudely off. “Little men’s redes, foster-father, shall not serve my turn. ’Tis not in my blood.” He spoke, as was ever the way of him, with a little stuttering here and there, as if the great and eager spirit of him in its haste tripped and stumbled over the slowness of his speech.

“No jot less of that blood,” answered the Earl, shouting in his ear for the wind sake, “runneth in thine uncle’s veins. He loveth thee. Wilt thou bite the hand that feeds thee? Go in with me. Or why neededst thou put that shaming on free men, to put the name of thrall upon them?”

Styrbiorn leapt up a-laughing. “Did Aki’s nose swell at that?” he cried. “Of age, saidst thou! Come off, and back me.”

“Hold,” said the Earl. But the lad was away: three bounds down the steep to the rough pasture, and away toward the King’s hall. Earl Wolf was a handy man, but he scarce overtook him in the great hall door.

===Chinese===
斯蒂尔比约恩一动也不动。他裹着一件细织的紫色呢绒斗篷，缘边用黑线绣着繁丽的纹样。任他裹得再严实，斗篷仍旧鼓荡翻飞，像风暴中断了舵的船帆。他头上无帽，一头厚厚的黄色短卷发应和着风的呼哨跳跃，恰如坟上的茂草。他颈间戴一条足金的粗项圈，金质柔腻，弯作弧形贴在颈肩相接之处，以金工之艺镂满华丽的嵌饰，两端各有一枚龙首。他双手托着下巴，逆风蹙眉，眯得双眼流泪。

伯爵坐下来，伸臂搂住他。“王并没有扣住你的产业，斯蒂尔比约恩。他已经许了诺，也必会给你，这你深知。只是时候未到。你如今才十五岁。”

斯蒂尔比约恩粗鲁地甩开他。“义父，小人物的算计于我无用。我的血里没有这个。”他说话时——这也素来是他的常态——处处带着些许口吃，仿佛他那宏大而急切的心魂在前头赶路，却被自己迟缓的言语绊得踉踉跄跄。

“那样的血，”伯爵迎着风在他耳边喊道，“在你舅舅的血管里流的也分毫不差。他爱你。你难道要咬那喂养你的手么？跟我进去吧。还有，你何苦那样羞辱自由人，把奴仆之名扣在他们头上？”

斯蒂尔比约恩放声大笑，一跃而起。“阿基的鼻子被这话气肿了么？”他喊道，“说什么年纪！下来，给我助阵。”

“站住，”伯爵说。但少年早已去了：三个纵步跳下陡坡，落到崎岖的牧场上，直奔王的厅堂而去。沃尔夫伯爵是个利落人，也几乎追不上他，直到大厅门口方才赶上。

===Original===
Lamps were kindled in the hall, and the ruddy firelight mingling with their colder beams pulsed and flickered, betwixt rush-strewn floor and the uncertain darkness of the roof-timbers, on bench and board and the many-coloured raiment of that great company and worshipful that there were set. Eric the King sat in his high seat on the upper bench. That other on the lower bench over against him was empty. He wore a Greek cloak of scarlet silken stuff and a blue kirtle done about with needlework. Gold rings that weighed twelve ounces each were on his arms above the elbows, and a crown of fine gold was on his head. King Eric was of all men the fairest to look on; and for all he was well nigh threescore winters old he was neither bent nor wrinkled, but fresh-looking and stark and stalwart as any man in his prime age, and his hair and beard most fair and thick, albeit something dashed with gray, and the voice of him deep and strong and pleasant to hear, and his eyes of a gray colour and speckled, and very bright.

Styrbiorn came and stood before the King betwixt the fire and the board. The King pointed to the high seat over against him, and said, “Take thy place, kinsman.”

Styrbiorn eyed him squarely, then answered and said in his hasty stuttering way, “That was in my mind, King, that I should be no more a burden unto you nor a kept person, now that I am come to man’s estate. In token whereof, I would not come to your board today, but abode by my father’s howe.”

“ ’Tis wearisome,” said the King, “if this old mummery must be acted anew each year. Imagine my part spoke: so, ’tis done. And now, no more on’t.”

Styrbiorn said, “If, King, you will do me right now. Render up to me my father’s heritage: that share of the lordship of the Swedes which belonged to the King my father. Then I will sit in yonder seat. But not otherwise. Not as your guest, King.”

“Kinsman,” said the King, “thou art, of all folk I ever did know, man or woman, the most thrawart and stubborn of will. I say unto thee, as last year I said and the year before last: when thou shalt be sixteen winters old I will give thee thine inheritance.”

===Chinese===
厅中灯火已燃，红红的火光与灯盏清冷的光交混，在灯芯草铺就的地席与屋顶梁木的幽暗之间明灭摇曳，照着条凳与食案，照着在座那一大群尊贵宾客的五彩衣袍。埃里克王坐在上席的高座上。对面下席与之相对的那个高座空着。他身披一领猩红丝绸的希腊式斗篷，内着绣工镶边的蓝色长衣。双臂肘上各戴着一只十二盎司重的金环，头上戴着赤金王冠。埃里克王是所有人中最俊美的一位；虽年近六旬，腰不弯，面无皱，气色鲜明，筋骨挺拔，壮健如盛年之人；须发至美至密，只是微染霜色；嗓音深沉洪亮，闻之悦耳；眼睛灰色，点缀斑点，极是明亮。

斯蒂尔比约恩走上前来，立在火池与食案之间、王的面前。王指着对面那个高座说：“坐到你的位子上去，亲人。”

斯蒂尔比约恩直直地看着他，然后以他急促而口吃的方式答道：“王，我心里正是这么想的：如今我已成年，不该再做你的累赘、受你供养。为了作证，今日我才不来赴你的席，只守在我父亲的坟旁。”

“真叫人厌烦，”王说，“这出旧戏竟要年年重演。就当我那部分的戏词念过了：如此，戏完了。此事休再提。”

斯蒂尔比约恩说：“王，你若肯现在还我公道：把我父亲的遗产交还给我——那属于我父王的、瑞典治权中的一份。那么我便坐到那边那个座位上去。否则休想。我不做你的宾客，王。”

“亲人，”王说，“我平生所识之人，无论男女，数你最执拗、最倔强。我告诉你，如同去年与前年说过的一样：等你满十六岁，我必把你的产业给你。”

===Original===
“Waxed is the bairn, but not the breeks,” said Styrbiorn, and his face was red as blood.

“When thou shalt be sixteen winters old,” said the King. “Till then, be quiet. For stubborn-set as thou art, I am as stubborn; and I, not thou, will rule in this matter, as is but just and right.”

“Seldom recovers Kings their dominions,” said Styrbiorn fiercely, “but with hewing of swords.” But when he had spoken those words he looked up at the King his uncle and met his gaze; and there was that in the King’s countenance that stayed his violent mood, as a draught of cool water in the mouth stays the burning of an over-hot mouthful of meat. There was fallen a sudden silence in the hall, for all that men were well feasted and much game and jesting was toward. And Styrbiorn, that had been red with anger, flushed yet darker even to his brow and neck; and he stood shamefaced before the King. He said in a low voice, “Some devil drew the tongue out of my mouth to speak an ill word. And now I will sit at your board, as well it befitteth I should do. But if it please you, King, I will come and talk more hereof tomorrow.”

“Let us give thanks,” said the King, “for a little respite.”

The cup had by then gone many a time about the tables, and men’s bellies were well bulked with ale and their wise discretion and judgement something befogged withal. And as in such a season a man will oft say that which tumbleth quickliest to his tongue, so Aki of the King’s bodyguard (bethinking him not at all that he should as well tickle a wolf under the chin as fret Styrbiorn now) plucked him by the kirtle as he came walking by the lower bench, and asked when he should have amends for the scurvy words Styrbiorn that day had given him.

“Hold thy tongue, King’s thrall,” said Styrbiorn; and, with the word, cast off the cloak from his shoulders and smothered it over Aki’s head. Now Aki had in his hand a great silver-rimmed drinking-horn all full of ale, and it spilt all down the neck of him. He leapt up and cast away the cloak and drave the horn at Styrbiorn’s nose so that the blood gushed out of his nose and he staggered back and well nigh into the fire. Aki ran out, but Styrbiorn overtook him in the doorway and caught him by the collar and jerked him backwards. Young as he was, Styrbiorn had yet the strength of an ordinary man full-grown. He was mad wroth, and he drove the man down under him with fist and knee. Aki had by now pulled out a sax-knife and aimed to smite him withal. Styrbiorn rived it out of his hand and drove it into Aki’s neck and down into the man’s body, clean up to the heft of the knife. Aki needed no other blow but was dead at once.

===Chinese===
“孩子长大了，裤子却没长，”斯蒂尔比约恩说，脸红得像血。

“等你满十六岁，”王说，“在那之前，安分些。你固然一根死筋，我也一样一根死筋；这件事由我做主，不由你，这才是公道正理。”

“君王夺回自己的疆土，”斯蒂尔比约恩狠声道，“少有不靠刀剑砍出来的。”话一出口，他抬眼望着他的舅舅、望着王，正与王的目光相接；王的面容里有一种东西止住了他的暴戾，如一口凉水止住了满口烫得过热的肉块的灼烧。厅中霎时一片肃静——尽管众人酒足饭饱，正要行令戏谑。方才怒得满脸通红的斯蒂尔比约恩，此刻涨得更红了，一直红到额头和脖颈；他满面羞惭地立在王前。他低声说：“有魔鬼拽出了我的舌头，教我说了歹话。如今我愿入席就座，这本是合乎我身份的。王，你若愿意，我明日再来同你细谈此事。”

“让我们谢天谢地，”王说，“总算有片刻清静。”

那时角杯已在各席间传了又传，众人腹中灌满了麦酒，聪明的审慎与判断也随之昏了几分。人在这种时节，出口的往往是最先滚到舌尖上的话。于是王的卫士阿基——全然想不到此刻去撩斯蒂尔比约恩，就好比去搔狼的下巴——见他沿着下席走来，便扯住他的长衣，问他几时才能讨个公道，为斯蒂尔比约恩白日里给他的那些下贱话。

“住口，王的奴才，”斯蒂尔比约恩说；话音未落，已甩脱肩上的斗篷，兜头罩住阿基。阿基手里本擎着一只银箍大角杯，满斟麦酒，酒尽数泼进他的颈项。他跳将起来，甩开斗篷，把角杯直撞在斯蒂尔比约恩的鼻梁上，血顿时从鼻中涌出，他踉跄后退，几乎跌进火里。阿基夺门而逃，斯蒂尔比约恩却在门口赶上他，揪住他的衣领，猛地把他向后一拽。斯蒂尔比约恩年纪虽轻，力气却不亚于寻常成年壮汉。他怒极如狂，用拳头和膝盖把那人压倒在身下。阿基这时已拔出一柄撒克短刀，要来刺他。斯蒂尔比约恩把刀夺出手，直刺进阿基的脖颈，深入身体，直到刀柄为止。阿基不必再挨第二天，当场毙命。

===Original===
Now was turmoil in the King’s hall, and much carping and high talk this way and that; for Aki was a man of good kin among the bonders, and many were there who would have done vengeance for him on Styrbiorn without more ado, but that the place and the King’s majesty put them in awe. In the end was all quiet again, and the dead corpse carried out, and men fell again to drinking, yet something less blithely than afore.

The next morning was Styrbiorn up betimes and betook him once more toward his father’s howe. For, albeit he was well agreed with the King his uncle after last night’s doings to sit quiet yet another year and thereafter be received into kingdom, yet him-seemed there should be no place where he should be more at ease when he had naught to do than there on the howe.

There was a spring in his step as he walked. He went not straight to the howe but took a sweep out into the open country, looking this way and that as searching for something. He was come within about a hundred paces from the howe when he espied that whereof he stood in need: a dark and shaggy body, four-legged, sturdy, high-shouldered, tailless, with a marvellous long and hairy coat that trailed well nigh to the big cloven hooves of the beast, and with great curly horns like a ram’s, and hairy face and nose, grazing some way off from him in the rough pastures that went down to Fyriswater. Styrbiorn halted on his way and gave a low call. The thing stopped grazing, lifted head and looked and saw him. It stood still at gaze: he called again. It lifted its nose and gave an answering bellow; then, like a coyly playful girl that will be wooed but must provoke, turned about and walked away from him, casting a look behind every four or five paces to make sure it was being followed. Styrbiorn was upon it ere it had gone twenty yards, and caught it by the horns. “Moldi,” he said, “how durst thou, when I want thee?” It was a yearling musk-ox, littler than common oxen, but heavy enough and strong enough to do with most men as it would; but with Styrbiorn, as was well seen as they tussled and strove together, it was very nicely matched. Round and round they swung for a while, trampling the ground, swaying and staggering back and forth, puffing and snorting both the one and the other, till Styrbiorn broke off the game and leaped backward several paces and there stood panting and laughing, facing his little ox. “Come on,” he shouted; and it put down its head and charged. Styrbiorn braced his whole frame to bear the onset, and by the sheer strength of him stood his ground. This bout they played over not twice nor thrice but a dozen times; wherein three times was Styrbiorn thrown, but all other times he was victor in their encounter. But whenever Styrbiorn was thrown, his little ox was careful not to trample nor hurt him, but shoved its nose into his face, breathing its sweet breath all about him, and then stood back for the charge as soon as he was gotten on his feet again. At length they stopped both. Styrbiorn sat on the ground propped on his two hands behind him, breathing hard. Moldi stood over him, nuzzling his hairy nose into Styrbiorn’s neck between jaw and collarbone. Styrbiorn rubbed his cheek on Moldi’s nose like a cat. In a minute he stood up. “To the howe,” he said. Moldi turned and set off the contrary way. Styrbiorn caught him, and with less violent tussling now, (for they were now both well blown and desired no longer sinew-testing in earnest but some show of it only to mind them of their sport), half dragged half coaxed him to the howe. There they rested together an hour or more, the one fitting heads to his arrows, the other chewing the cud. A mile or more eastward, on their left, rose the scree-slopes of Windbergsfell: southaway from beneath their feet wound Fyriswater amid the flats below Upsala, and there lay spread the far-stretching low country toward Sigtun and the sea, and westward the far mountains of Upland shadowy-gray in the light of morning.

When Moldi had taken his fill of chewing the cud, he stood up and butted Styrbiorn gently from behind. Styrbiorn sprang up and caught him by the horns, and again they fought and wrastled on the howe top, till Styrbiorn forced Moldi’s head round sideways and threw him down and held him there by main force, his face against his. A long time they lay so, Moldi ever and again putting forth spasmodic efforts to rise, Styrbiorn holding him down with all his might and laughing the while in his brown and furry ear.

At length Moldi lay quiet, as if to grant he was mastered as for this time. Styrbiorn let go, and rolled over on his back. His eyes were closed, his great and shapely arms flung out a-stretch on either side, his right hand burrowing and fondling among the warm soft depths of woolly hair under Moldi’s jowl, his left opening and shutting as if to ease the stiffness in his fingers after so much clutching on the horns. His chest, broad and deep as few men’s of full age, mounted and sank with slow regular and profound breaths. So he lay, with shut eyes and with lips parted a little like a dreamer’s who smiles in his sleep; and all the while knew not who was stolen up quietly behind him on the howe, and had all the while stood there looking down on that rough and tumble, the hard panting of boy and ox, the splendour of Styrbiorn’s strong limbs with every sinew strained and hard in the struggle: and stood there still, watching him in silence.

A tall lass she was, standing there over him, her dark gown gathered in one hand so that her shapely ankles showed below it. Her hair, tawny red, braided with gold cords into two thick trammels, reached to her knee at either side. High-bosomed she was, light of flank, clean-limbed, and with somewhat of almost manly presence and stature, yet graceful beyond all telling. The carriage of her was like the dragonhead of a ship of war treading turbulent seas, and the face of her (albeit she was yet scarce grown woman), of that kind and seeming which belike Queen Brynhild’s had of old, or Gudrun’s of Laxriverdale, and other women’s faces that were born to be the bane of men.

===Chinese===
王的厅中登时大乱，斥责声、吵嚷声四起；原来阿基是自由农中良家出身，在场不少人恨不得立时找斯蒂尔比约恩报仇，只是碍于这地点、碍于王的威仪，心存忌惮。末了一切复归平静，尸身抬了出去，众人重新落座饮酒，只是兴致不如方才了。

次日清晨，斯蒂尔比约恩早早起身，又往他父亲的坟冢那边去了。虽说经过昨夜之事，他已同他舅舅王讲和，答应再安分一年、届时入朝共治，但他总觉得，闲来无事之时，世上再没有比那坟冢上更叫他自在的地方了。

他一路步履轻快。他没有直奔坟冢，而是绕到开阔的野地里，东张西望，像在寻找什么。走到离坟冢约百步之遥，他望见了他正要寻的东西：一团黑黝黝毛茸茸的躯体，四足，矮壮，肩高背隆，没有尾巴，一身出奇的长毛几乎拖到那双大的偶蹄上，双角卷曲如公羊，脸上鼻上尽是绒毛，正在下方不远处通往菲里斯河的崎岖牧场上吃草。斯蒂尔比约恩停住脚步，低唤了一声。那东西止了吃草，抬头张望，看见了他。它定定地凝望；他又唤了一声。它昂起鼻子，应声哞叫；随即像个娇俏贪玩的姑娘——明知要人求，偏要先撩人——转过身去，背着他走开，每走四五步便回头望一眼，看看有没有人跟来。它没走出二十码，斯蒂尔比约恩已扑到跟前，一把抓住双角。“莫尔迪，”他说，“我要用你，你敢躲？”那是一头一岁口的麝牛，比寻常的牛小些，却够重、够壮，寻常人多半由它摆布；可与斯蒂尔比约恩——两个扭作一团时看得分明——却正是旗鼓相当。他们兜着圈子旋转，践踏地面，前仰后合，踉踉跄跄，一人一牛都在喷鼻息、喘粗气，直到斯蒂尔比约恩歇了这游戏，向后跃开几步，立在那里喘息着、大笑着，面对他的小牛。“来吧，”他喊道；小牛低下头冲将过来。斯蒂尔比约恩扎稳全身的架子迎击，凭着一身蛮力岿然不动。这一合他们玩了不止两回、不止三回，而是十几回；其中三回斯蒂尔比约恩被掀翻在地，其余各番交手都是他赢。但每逢他被掀翻，小牛总小心着不踩他、不伤他，只把鼻子拱进他脸上，朝他喷吐香甜的气息，然后退开，单等他重新爬起，再行冲撞。末了两个都住了手。斯蒂尔比约恩坐在地上，两手撑在身后，喘个不停。莫尔迪立在他上方，把毛鼻子拱进他的颈窝、颌骨与锁骨之间。斯蒂尔比约恩像猫一样用脸颊蹭莫尔迪的鼻子。片刻他站起身。“上冢去，”他说。莫尔迪却转过身，偏往相反的方向去。斯蒂尔比约恩追上它，这一回扭斗得不那么凶了（两个都已气喘吁吁，不想再认真较力，只想比划比划，好记得这游戏的滋味），半拖半哄地把它弄上了坟冢。他们在那里一同歇息了个把时辰，一个给箭配镞，一个倒嚼回草。东边一英里开外，左手方向，温德贝格山乱石嶙嶙的山坡耸起；南面，菲里斯河自他们脚下蜿蜒流过乌普萨拉下方的平川；低地铺展开去，直抵锡格蒂纳与大海；西边，乌普兰的远山在晨光中一片灰蒙蒙的暗影。

莫尔迪倒嚼够了，站起身来，从背后轻轻顶了斯蒂尔比约恩一下。斯蒂尔比约恩一跃而起，抓住牛角，两个又在坟顶上扭斗起来，直到斯蒂尔比约恩把莫尔迪的头拧向一旁，将它掀翻按住，脸对着脸。他们这样躺了许久，莫尔迪不时猛地挣扎着想爬起，斯蒂尔比约恩拼尽全力按住它，一边对着它那棕色毛茸茸的耳朵笑。

末了莫尔迪安静躺下了，仿佛认了输，这一回合甘拜下风。斯蒂尔比约恩松开手，仰面翻滚躺倒。他双眼闭着，修长健美的双臂向两侧直直摊开，右手埋在莫尔迪颌下羊毛般长毛温暖柔软的深处抚弄，左手一开一合，仿佛在缓释久抓牛角后指节的僵直。他的胸膛宽阔深厚，成年人也少有及得上的，随着缓慢、匀整而深沉的呼吸起伏。他就这样躺着，闭着眼，嘴唇微微张开，像睡梦中微笑的做梦人；始终不曾察觉坟上有人蹑手蹑脚从背后走近，一直站在那里俯瞰这场翻滚缠斗——少年与牛的粗重喘息，斯蒂尔比约恩强健四肢的光彩，每一条筋腱在搏斗中绷紧如铁——而此刻依然站在那里，默默注视着他。

那是一个高挑的少女，立在他上方，一手提起深色裙裾，露出下面秀美的脚踝。她的头发赤褐色，编着金线结成两条粗大的发辫，垂到两侧膝头。她胸脯丰满，腰身轻捷，四肢匀净，气度身量几乎带着几分男子般的轩昂，却又妩媚得难以言传。她的仪态，如战船的龙首破开汹涌的海面；她的面容（虽说尚是没长成的女子）属于那一类品貌——想必古时的布伦希尔德王后、拉克司河谷的古德伦，以及那些生来便是男人克星的女人们，当年所有的便是这类模样。

===Original===
But this was not the lady Gudrun come from Iceland, nor yet Brynhild, Budli’s daughter, returned from her Hell-ride in these latter days and back from the dead, that stood with proud grave mouth and unsounded dark-brown eyes gazing on the might of Styrbiorn while he struggled with Moldi on his father’s howe; but Sigrid, Skogul-Tosti’s daughter out of Arland, the mightiest and noblest of all men of Sweden who lacked title of dignity, and she some few days since ridden from home with her father for feasting and guesting with King Eric Biornson.

Opening his eyes at last and seeing her, Styrbiorn sat up a little sullen-looking and shamefaced and gave her greeting. He gave Moldi a flick on the nose with the back of his hand, who forthwith lumbered up to his feet and down from the howe and away.

“This is a strange sport,” said Sigrid. “What manner of cow is this?”

“ ’Tis not a cow,” said Styrbiorn. “ ’Tis my bull. He cometh from the northlands, many days’ journey beyond Helsingland. His name is Moldi.”

He began tinkering again with his arrows, fitting heads to them. Sigrid watched, bending over his shoulder from behind. Styrbiorn took no more note of her than if she had been his musk-ox. “Shall I sit here awhile?” she said at length.

“As thou wilt,” answered he.

===Chinese===
但站在那里的，并不是从冰岛来的古德伦夫人，也不是布德利之女布伦希尔德——在这迟来的岁月里从冥府之旅归来、从死者中还阳——以倨傲端庄的嘴唇和深不见底的深棕色眼睛，注视斯蒂尔比约恩在他父亲坟上与莫尔迪角力时的雄威；站在那里的是西格丽德，出自阿兰德的斯科古尔-托斯蒂的女儿——那托斯蒂，在瑞典凡没有尊爵头衔的人当中，是最强大、最高贵的一位——她几天前随父亲离家出门，来埃里克·比约恩森王这里饮宴作客。

斯蒂尔比约恩终于睁眼看见了她，坐起身来，面色有些阴郁羞赧，向她问了好。他用手背在莫尔迪鼻子上拍了一下，那牛便笨重地爬起来，下了坟冢，自去了。

“这游戏倒新鲜，”西格丽德说，“这是头什么样的母牛？”

“不是母牛，”斯蒂尔比约恩说，“是我的公牛。它从北边来，过了赫尔辛兰还要再走许多天的路程。它叫莫尔迪。”

他又开始捣鼓他的箭，给箭装镞。西格丽德从后面俯在他肩上看。斯蒂尔比约恩对她的在意，不多过对他的麝牛。“我在这儿坐一会儿行么？”她终于开口。

“随你的便，”他答道。

===Original===
Sigrid sat down beside him with the grace of a seabird alighting on the wave. His shoulder was turned towards her. He went on with his work. Herself unobserved, she sat there quiet, looking on what he did, but most on him.

“Arrows is women’s weapons,” said Styrbiorn after a while, looking along the shaft to see he had fitted it true. “I know not why I fash myself with such things.”

Sigrid said nothing, watching the ripple of the muscles beneath his skin of arms and neck and shoulder, the great clean-modelled knees, and the yellow hair of his head so close and thick.

“It is a wonder thou shouldst like to sit all by thyself in this place,” she said.

He made no answer. She was so near that the breath of her, sweet like kine’s breath, mixed with his own.

“There be few men so strong as to hold down that bull,” said she.

===Chinese===
西格丽德在他身旁坐下，姿态轻盈，如海鸟落在浪上。他的肩膀偏向一旁，背着她。他继续干他的活。她无人理会，静静坐在那里，看他做的事，但更多是看他。

“箭是女人的兵器，”过了一会儿斯蒂尔比约恩说，眼睛顺着箭杆望过去，看镞装得正不正，“不知我为何拿这些玩意儿烦自己。”

西格丽德一言不发，看他手臂、脖颈和肩头皮肤下肌肉的起伏，看他那干净利落的大膝盖，看他头上那一头又密又厚的黄发。

“真叫人奇怪，你竟喜欢独自一人坐在这地方，”她说。

他没有作声。她坐得那么近，她的气息，甜得像牛的气息，与他的气息混在了一处。

“能按得住那头公牛的人，可没有几个，”她说。

===Original===
“He getteth me down now and then,” said Styrbiorn.

Sigrid’s shoulder touched his, lightly as a moth. He shifted away a little, laying down one arrow and choosing another. She shifted too, the other way. Her face flamed red on a sudden, and turned fierce and hard so as it was a wonder to see in a girl so young and tender. For a long time they were silent. Then she said, “I did never see a man slain till last night.”

“Wast afeared to see it?” asked Styrbiorn.

“Not afeared,” she said. “Thou hast ta’en to the work young.” She was looking at him somewhat strangely, her eyes a-sparkle.

“I slew him not sackless,” said Styrbiorn. “ ’Would have stabbed me first. Thou knewest that?”

“I knew it truly,” she answered.

===Chinese===
“它时不时也把我掀翻，”斯蒂尔比约恩说。

西格丽德的肩膀碰了碰他的，轻得像一只飞蛾。他挪开了一点，放下这支箭，另取一支。她也挪了挪，往另一边。她的脸霎时烧红了，变得又凶又硬，在这样年轻娇嫩的姑娘脸上见到这般神色，真叫人吃惊。两人沉默了许久。然后她说：“直到昨夜以前，我从没见过杀人。”

“见了害怕么？”斯蒂尔比约恩问。

“不害怕，”她说，“你干这行当，上手倒早。”她看着他，眼神有些异样，双目闪闪发亮。

“我杀他并非无端，”斯蒂尔比约恩说，“是他要先捅我。这你知道？”

“我确实知道，”她答道。

===Original===
“And I’ll pay no boot for his slaying,” said Styrbiorn, turning to look her in the eye. “I’ll learn these bonders’ sons to bear them more quietly in kings’ houses,” said he.

She said nothing.

“It is good to be a king,” said Styrbiorn after a little.

Sigrid seemed as if she heard him not. She was gazing with an altered countenance south over Fyrisfield. Styrbiorn looked up and saw her so stare, wide-eyed, as if in fear: as if in those silent water-meadows she saw some strange matter, hidden until now.

“Wouldst not thou be a queen, Sigrid,” said he, “and ’twere offered thee?”

Still she spoke not. A cloud in that instant hid the sun. The girl shivered.

===Chinese===
“这人的命，我也不出赎金，”斯蒂尔比约恩说着，转过身直视她的眼睛，“我要教教这些自由农的儿子们，在王者之家怎样放规矩些。”他说。

她没有说话。

“做国王是件美事，”过了片刻，斯蒂尔比约恩说。

西格丽德仿佛没有听见。她神色一变，朝南望着菲里斯原野。斯蒂尔比约恩抬头，见她睁大眼睛那样凝望，像是怀着恐惧：仿佛在那片寂静的水草滩上，她看见了什么至今隐藏着的怪事。

“西格丽德，若是有人把王后的位子送到你面前，”他说，“你难道不愿做王后么？”

她仍不作声。恰在此时，一片云遮住了太阳。姑娘哆嗦了一下。

===Original===
“Wouldst thou not?” said Styrbiorn.

“Not what?” said she, shivering again and turning to look at him. “I marked not what thou saidst.”

“Be a queen?” said Styrbiorn.

“Yes,” she said.

“Why dost thou look so, as if thou hadst seen something?”

“ ’Tis nothing,” she said. But seeing in his eyes that her looks belied her words, “Nothing,” said she again. “Thou art but a child, Styrbiorn, for all that thou didst slay a man last night.”

===Chinese===
“你不愿意么？”斯蒂尔比约恩说。

“什么？”她说，又哆嗦了一下，转过头来看他，“我没留神你方才说了什么。”

“做王后？”斯蒂尔比约恩说。

“嗯，”她说。

“你为什么这副神色，像看见了什么东西似的？”

“没什么，”她说。但见他眼里的神色，知道自己的脸色拆穿了自己的话，便又说了一遍：“没什么。斯蒂尔比约恩，你不过是个孩子，纵然你昨夜杀了一个人。”

===Original===
“Something too childishly,” said he, scowling, “goeth mine affair. It needed not thee too, to thrust that down my throat.”

Sigrid shivered and said, “Come from this place. I am three years older than thou, and can see things thou canst not see. The dead be in this place. Come away.”

But Styrbiorn budged not from his seat. He took another arrow, then smiling scornfully said to her, “Thou art a woman, Sigrid. Women are ever afeared of bugs and bogles. The living be in this place: thou and I: not the dead. And well I love this place. ’Tis a place of Kings. And if any dead man be hereabout, it is the King my father.”

He looked up at her again. Her eyes were fixed on him, but as if she saw him not. She looked ghastly. He leaped up and took her by the arm, being bit with remorse a little for his churlishness, and a little touched with the infection of her strange speechless dread; even as it is a man of rare coolness, who, sitting alone with his dog in a desolate house at nighttime, seeth the dog stare and growl as if at some unseen presence in the room and feeleth in himself no answering tremor. “Come,” he said, “I’ll go in with thee. There’s naught to fear. Come.”

===Chinese===
“我的事办得有点太孩子气了，”他皱着眉说，“可也用不着连你都来拿这个堵我的嘴。”

西格丽德哆嗦着说：“离开这地方吧。我比你大三岁，能看见你看不见的东西。这地方有死人。走吧。”

但斯蒂尔比约恩坐着不动。他又拿起一支箭，随即带着轻蔑的笑对她说：“你是个女人，西格丽德。女人总是怕虫怕鬼。这地方有的是活人：你和我：没有死人。我深爱这地方。这是王者的地方。这附近纵然真有什么死人，那也是我的父王。”

他又抬头看她。她的双眼定在他身上，却像并没有看见他。她的脸色惨白如鬼。他跳起来，挽住她的手臂——他为自己方才的粗鲁有些懊悔，也被她那无由的、无言的恐惧感染了一二；正如一个素来极冷静的人，夜里独自带着狗坐在荒宅之中，见狗对着屋里某个看不见的东西瞪视咆哮，自己心里却泛不起一丝相应的战栗。“来，”他说，“我陪你进去。没什么可怕的。来吧。”

## II / 二

===Original===
Thorgnyr the Lawman

Now there was mighty discontent among the bonder-folk because of this slaying of Aki, and much murmuring against Styrbiorn and his lawless and unbridled vein who should so slay the man and pay no boot therefor. In the end the King himself did boot it, and so these growls died down for the while.

When it was spring, the King fared north into the coasted parts of Helsingland and into Jarnberaland a-guesting, and it was full summer when he came home, riding with them of his bodyguard down to the high arm of the firth over against Sigtun. It was a windy day of driving mist that made gray and ghostly the whole face of the countryside, blotting out the hills and woods and confounding water and sky in the same hue and tone of pale grey without colour; only the water was darkened with the little shadows innumerable of the hastening waves, and here and there a reef showed, darker than aught else visible, and against it the flash of breakers that leapt and fell. Like swooping birds, black squalls swooped and chased one another, scurrying in zigzags far and wide, doubling and turning, always with little glass-smooth strips of calm water on the edges of the squalls. And there were swift changing markings made by the wind and tide, as it were a white sword and a black that crossed edges on the troubled surface of the loch. A man on a dark horse came riding up from the waterside to meet the King. The King knew him for Earl Wolf.

“Thy colour looketh trouble,” said the King. “What’s the tidings?”

The Earl told him in his ear, as one who disburdeneth himself of a weighty matter and a grievous and feareth the while lest evil betide him, as sometimes it betideth to those who tell bad news to kings. A pretty tale in truth the Earl had to tell: of a Thing summoned in Upsala against all law, the King being not there; and at the Thing fierce disputes betwixt the bonder-folk of the one part and Styrbiorn and his friends of the other, with all the grief of Aki’s slaying dug up anew and all their old counts against Styrbiorn, wherein at last Styrbiorn said loudly that he was, of his father’s right, King of half the realm of Sweden, and they should learn it to their cost. Whereupon, great uproar among the bonders, who in fine carried it with so high a hand that they feared not to choose and proclaim one Lambi the White, a man from Stocksound in Tenthland, King in Styrbiorn’s despite of that half of the kingdom claimed by him; and therewith the Thing broken up in a tumult, so that it had wanted but a little to have befallen a battle betwixt the King’s men and the bonders.

The King heard him out, and was silent for a little space, his countenance clouded. At length, “It agreeth i’ the main,” said he, “with that which I knew hereof already.”

===Chinese===
法律宣讲人松格尼尔

如今因这杀死阿基之事，自由农众中大为不满，对斯蒂尔比约恩怨声四起——他这样杀了人，竟不赔赎金，无法无天，全无管束。末了是王自己出资赔结了此事，这些怨言才一时平息下去。

开春之后，王北上往赫尔辛兰沿海各地与亚恩贝拉兰做客游历；待他归来已是盛夏，与卫士们策马下到正对锡格蒂纳的湾口高岸。那是一个刮风的日子，飞驰的浓雾把整个乡野涂得灰暗如鬼域，山峦林木一概抹去，水天混沌，同作一片无色的苍白；只有水面被疾奔浪头无数细碎的暗影点染得发黑，四处偶有礁石露出，比一切可见之物都黑，浪花在礁上迸溅明灭，跳起又跌落。黑色的狂飙如俯冲的飞鸟，追逐竞逐，远远近近地蛇形疾走，回旋转折，狂风边缘总伴着一道道玻璃般光滑的静水。风与潮在湖面扰动的水上留下飞快变换的印痕，仿佛一柄白剑与一柄黑剑交相切划。一个骑黑马的人从水边迎着王驰来。王认得那是沃尔夫伯爵。

“你的脸色像是有事，”王说，“有什么消息？”

伯爵凑在他耳边低声禀报，如一个人卸下沉重而凶险的心事，一面又惴惴不安，惟恐大祸落到自己头上——向君王报凶信的人，有时便遭此祸。伯爵带来的实在是个“妙”故事：有人在乌普萨拉违反一切法度擅自召开民众大会，其时王不在那里；会上自由农一方与斯蒂尔比约恩及其朋友们一方激烈争执，杀死阿基的旧恨被重新翻出，他们把对斯蒂尔比约恩的一切陈年旧账又数了一遍；末了斯蒂尔比约恩高声宣布：凭他父亲的权利，他是半个瑞典王国的王，他们必将自食苦果才肯明白。于是自由农大哗，最终竟敢肆无忌惮，公然推举并拥立一个白人兰比——丁特兰斯托克松德地方的人——为王，硬夺斯蒂尔比约恩所索的那半个王国；大会遂在骚乱中散场，差一点便要酿成王的人马与自由农之间的一场厮杀。

王听他讲完，沉默了片刻，面色阴沉。末了说道：“这与我先前所知的，大体相符。”

===Original===
“You did already know?” said the Earl, much wondering.

“Many are the King’s ears. Was Thorgnyr not there to stop them when they made this fair election?” asked the King.

“There was he, it is most certain,” said Earl Wolf. “But he did little enough to stop them.”

“I do trust Thorgnyr in all things else,” said the King, “but not in aught that toucheth Styrbiorn. He hath been ever against him.”

The Earl was silent.

“Was Thorgnyr there,” asked the King, “whenas they fell a-pelting of you with stones and muck to drive you from the Thing, thee and Styrbiorn?”

===Chinese===
“你早已知道了？”伯爵大为惊讶。

“王的耳目众多。他们行这桩‘美事’推举之时，松格尼尔难道不在场拦阻么？”王问。

“他在场，这是千真万确的，”沃尔夫伯爵说，“可他拦阻得少得可怜。”

“别的事我尽信得过松格尼尔，”王说，“唯独凡牵涉斯蒂尔比约恩的事信不过。他向来跟他作对。”

伯爵沉默不语。

“他们用石头和泥块砸你们、把你们轰出会场的时候——砸的是你和斯蒂尔比约恩——那时松格尼尔在场么？”王问。

===Original===
“You did know that too, King?” said the Earl. “I did think to have told you that later, not to light in a moment too hot a fire of anger in you. And yet, a pretty scout; a pretty shaming. ’Twere well to engrave the memory of those stones on the skin-coat of some of ’em.”

“Thou’st not yet answered me,” said King Eric: “was Thorgnyr in it when they stoned you from the Thing?”

“Lord, I’ll not lie to you,” answered the Earl; “he was not. O’ the contrary, I am apt to think that Thorgnyr, i’ the midst o’ this pother, was like naught so much as an unhandy cook, who, when he had brewed him this kettle full of trouble, did sit it over the fire until it boil, and did find too late he had not power in’s lean scragged arms to lift it off again afore all the mess should bubble over.”

“I must see Thorgnyr,” said the King. “Hast thou a boat here to put us across to Sigtun?”

The Earl brought him down to the water and showed him three ships, enough to take the King and all his company. They two stood together on the bank while the King’s men embarked. Earl Wolf looked angry as a man might be, biting of his moustachios and spitting out the hairs. The King, so long as he thought good, let him abide in this taking; then, wearying of the entertainment, “Let not fear shut thy mouth,” he said. “If I will hear Thorgnyr, how much more thee, which art blameless in this matter?”

“Have my thanks, King, for that,” said the Earl. “He is thy dog, I’ll not gainsay it. But here, where it toucheth Styrbiorn, will you see Thorgnyr? Will you hear this? How? this Lambi, this man of naught, this scurvy shagrag, chosen and proclaimed (flatly ’gainst the law) joint King in your noble young kinsman’s room? and will you see Thorgnyr? will you bandy words with him?”

===Chinese===
“那个你也知道了，王？”伯爵说，“我本想晚些再告诉你，免得一瞬间点着你过于炽烈的怒火。可这真是一遭好侦察，一场好羞辱。真该把那些石头的记性，刻到他们几个人的皮袄上。”

“你还没回答我，”埃里克王说：“他们用石头轰你们出会的时候，松格尼尔掺和了么？”

“主上，我不对你撒谎，”伯爵答道，“他没有。恰恰相反，我倒觉得，松格尼尔在那场乱子中间，活像个笨手笨脚的厨子：自己炖了满满一锅祸事，坐在火上把它煮开，却太晚发现凭他那两条干瘦的胳膊，没有力气把锅从炉上端下来，只好眼看着一锅脏东西扑腾着漫出来。”

“我必须见松格尼尔，”王说，“你这里有船渡我们去锡格蒂纳么？”

伯爵领他下到水边，指给他三艘船，足可载王与他全体随从。王的人登船之时，他们两个立在岸上。沃尔夫伯爵满面怒容，咬着髭须，又把须毛啐出来。王觉得有趣，便任他这般恼着；后来腻了这消遣，才说：“别让恐惧封住你的嘴。连松格尼尔的话我都肯听，何况你——你在这件事上并无过失。”

“这个多谢王了，”伯爵说，“他是你的一条狗，我不否认。可是如今事情牵到斯蒂尔比约恩，你竟要去见松格尼尔？你要听这个？怎么？这个兰比，这个废物，这个下三滥的破烂货，公然（全然违法地）被推举拥立，顶了你那位高贵年轻亲人的共治王之位？你还要去见松格尼尔？还要同他讲道理？”

===Original===
“Why wilt thou make such faces?” said the King.

“It was not so in Sweden aforetime,” said Earl Wolf, in a hot anger fanned by the breath of his own angry speech. “Did not King Ingiald Evil-heart formerly burn six kings in Upsala, ’cause he would have no man share his dominion? Can you for shame﻿—” but here he came to a full stop in his declaiming, having not so wholly suffered his indignation to master his sober senses that he should not mark that look in the King’s eye suddenly turned upon him, and be cowed by it to submission.

“To shame at that which is not shameworthy,” said the King after a minute, very quiet, “belongeth to a fool, not to a king. And thou, that hast been a famous skipper in thy time! Ay,” said the King: “that was well thought on. Yonder little cock-boat shall serve.” That was a little boat some fifteen foot long, that went with one of the big ships; and the King made the Earl go aboard of her and put off, they two alone. The King made Earl Wolf sit in the stern and steer, and made him hoist sail and steer for Sigtun. “Now I will see somewhat of thy seamanship,” said the King.

The Earl sailed handily, keeping his course as near as man could in that stormy weather; but ever and again, struck broadside by a charging squall, he must slack sail or throw her head into the wind.

“Is it as I think?” said the King, “that thy practice agreeth not with thy so loud babble? Make fast the sheet and keep her on a straight course to Sigtun, or it shall go ill with thee.” Nor would he harken at all to the Earl’s protestings, but threatened him with a great spear with an iron head a foot long; so that the Earl obeyed, and in a moment the boat was swamped and both King and Earl thrown into the water.

When they that were by the ships saw this they were put in a mighty stew and ran out a rowboat, but both King and Earl swam strongly and were come ashore before those others were well started out to rescue them. The King shook himself like a dog and fell a-laughing with great shouts of laughter. He clapped Earl Wolf on the shoulder, who stood there as a man not well knowing whether to laugh or be cross, wringing the water out of his breeches and kirtle. “Let me alone to rule Sweden,” said the King; “and do thou thy part to lesson thy foster-son with good and wholesome rede. And learn him not thy fashion of seamanship, but mine. For certain it is, there’s no shore to swim to, as here, whenas kingdoms are overset.”

===Chinese===
“你何必做出这副嘴脸？”王说。

“瑞典从前不是这样的，”沃尔夫伯爵说，自己的怒话像风箱，把他的怒火越扇越旺。“邪心王英吉亚德当年不是在乌普萨拉一把火烧了六个王，只因不愿任何人分他的权柄么？你难道不羞——”话到这里，他的慷慨陈词戛然而止：他的怒意毕竟没有全然压倒他清醒的神志，他看见王突然转向他的那种眼神，便被慑服，不敢造次了。

“为不该羞耻的事羞耻，”过了一分钟，王十分平静地说，“那是蠢人干的事，不是王者。你呢，当年还是个有名的船长！好啊，”王说：“这主意想得好。那边那只小舢板就够用了。”那是一条随大船同来的小船，长约十五英尺；王命伯爵登船解缆，只他们两个。王让沃尔夫伯爵坐在船尾掌舵，又命他扬帆，朝锡格蒂纳驶去。“现在我要看看你的船艺了。”王说。

伯爵驾得熟练，在那等风暴天里尽力稳住航向；却时不时被猛扑来的狂风拦腰一击，只得收帆，或调船头顶风。

“我猜得可对？”王说，“你的实干，配不上你那响亮的空谈？把帆索系紧，直朝锡格蒂纳开，不然有你好受的。”伯爵如何分辩，王全然不听，只拿一杆铁头足有一英尺长的大矛吓唬他；伯爵只得从命，一转眼船便灌水倾覆，王与伯爵双双落进水里。

船上众人见了，慌作一团，放下一只划艇；可王与伯爵都水性好，不等救援的人动身，早已上岸。王像狗一样抖了抖身子，放声大笑，笑声洪亮。他拍拍沃尔夫伯爵的肩膀——伯爵站在那里，不知该笑还是该恼，正拧着马裤和长衣里的水。“瑞典交给我一个人来治，”王说；“你呢，只管尽你的本分，用好言良言教导你的养子。船艺别教他你那一套，要教就教我这一套。要知道，王国倾覆的时候，可不像这里，有岸可以游过去。”

===Original===
---

Thorgnyr Thorgnyrson the Lawman came that same evening, obedient to the sending of Eric the King, to hold talk with him in a little fire-hall where the King was wont to sit when he would be private. The King made Thorgnyr sit on a low stool before the King’s feet. Old of years was Thorgnyr, and his beard both long and white, and his brow much furrowed, and with great shaggy eyebrows obscuring his eyes, and his cheeks hollow and lined, and his nose like an eagle’s beak. The head of him was bald.

The King said, “Fowls and beasts which herdeth and flocketh: is it to this pass thou wilt bring me the Swede-realm, Thorgnyr?”

Thorgnyr looked at him in silence for a moment. Then he answered and said, “To be free with you, King ’tis a younger than I and a nearer your own blood you should abraid with these unfortunes, not me.”

“So,” said the King: “sith old men’s hands grow feeble and let fall authority, we are to blame it on young blood that it runneth strong? We were best geld our young men, think’st thou, to make ’em docile, so as we may live out our time in quiet? or expose ’em all, and breed up girl-children only, that I and thou in our dotage may still find obedience?”

Thorgnyr bowed his head. “I marvel not, Lord, if you be angry. But if I wrought not my best for your interest so far as in me lay, then ask I no further thing at your hands than the loss of all I can lose: goods, lands, liberty, and last my life withal.”

===Chinese===
---

当天晚上，法律宣讲人松格尼尔·松格尼尔松遵埃里克王之召前来，与王在一间小小的火厅中叙话——王想清静独处时，惯常坐在这厅里。王让松格尼尔坐在自己脚前的矮凳上。松格尼尔年事已高，胡须又长又白，额上沟壑纵横，浓密蓬乱的长眉掩住了眼睛，双颊凹陷，布满皱纹，鼻如鹰喙。他的头顶已经秃了。

王说：“群飞的禽、成群的兽——松格尼尔，你要把我的瑞典国带到这步田地么？”

松格尼尔默然看了他片刻，然后答道：“恕我直言，王，这等祸事，您该去责备一个比我年轻、与您血脉更近的人，不该责备我。”

“如此说来，”王说，“既然老人的手软了、握不住掉下了权柄，就该怪年轻人的血气太旺？那依你说，我们最好把年轻人都阉了，好叫他们听话，让我们安安静静混完这辈子？或是把男婴都弃了，只养女孩，好叫你我到了老糊涂的年纪，仍有人孝顺？”

松格尼尔低下头。“您动怒，我不奇怪，主上。但若我没有竭尽所能为您的利益打算，那么我只求您一件事：取走我一切可失之物——财产、土地、自由，最后连我这条命。”

===Original===
“By whose authority,” said the King, “was this assembly holden, when the Thing was broken up according to law and I gone otherwhere for a season?”

“There was no authority for it, Lord,” answered he.

“Was it thy doing, Thorgnyr?” said the King.

He answered, “No.”

“Was it against thy strong withstanding?” said the King again.

“Lord, you must not press me over hard,” said Thorgnyr. “It was neither by my will nor counsel: that I can surely swear unto you.”

===Chinese===
“议会依法解散、我又暂离此地之后，”王说，“这场集会，是奉了谁的权柄开的？”

“并没有奉什么权柄，主上，”他答道。

“是你干的么，松格尼尔？”王说。

他答道：“不是。”

“你曾竭力反对么？”王又问。

“主上，您逼我莫要太紧，”松格尼尔说，“那既非出于我的意愿，也非出于我的谋划：这一点我可以向您起誓。”

===Original===
The King sat very still. Then he said, not raising his tone, yet with a note in his voice that menaced like a great dog’s growl, low and dangerous, “Is it come to this, that it must be tried out at last whether I or the bonders hath the lordship of this land of Sweden?”

The old man was silent, staring into the fire.

“I will have thine answer,” said the King.

Slowly Thorgnyr turned and looked in the King’s face. “Then answer me my question, King: Whether with the sun or with the rain ripeneth the corn unto harvest?”

Chin in hand, the King leaned over the arm of his carven chair, studying that old man where he sat bent in the dancing firelight, one pale fine hand tight-clutched across the other shoulder, holding close about him the gathered folds of his cloak of minivere, as if even by that hot fire his lean body was a-cold, the other clenched on his knee. After a while the King began to say, “Thou and I wax old. And when we are laid in howe the ordering of these things shall lie in other men’s hands, and they will order them as the Fates shall ordain. Belike it were a wise man’s part to let alone: what must be must be. Yet is that not my way. And besides, Thorgnyr,” said the King in an altered voice, “I do love this lad.”

After a pause, “Thou art silent,” said the King. “What dost think on?”

===Chinese===
王端坐不动。然后他开口了，声调并没有抬高，话音里却带着一种威胁，像大狗的低吼，低沉而危险：“事情竟到了这一步，终究要见个分晓：这瑞典国的宗主权，究竟在我，还是在自由农手里？”

老人默然，凝视着火。

“我必须要你的回答，”王说。

松格尼尔缓缓转过身来，看着王的脸。“那么请王先答我一个问题：田里的庄稼，是靠太阳晒熟的，还是靠雨水浇熟的？”

王以手托颐，俯在雕花扶手椅的扶手上，端详那老人：他佝偻着坐在跳动的火光里，一只苍白细瘦的手紧紧攥住另一边的肩头，把银鼠皮斗篷的褶皱拢在身上，仿佛即便守着这样旺的火，他瘦削的身体仍旧怕冷，另一只手攥在膝上。过了一会儿，王说道：“你我都在老去。等我们躺进坟冢，这些事的处置便落到旁人手里，他们会照命运所安排的去安排。顺其自然，兴许才是智者的本分：该来的总要来。可那不是我的路数。况且，松格尼尔，”王换了一种声音说，“我疼这孩子。”

停了片刻，“你不说话，”王说，“在想什么？”

===Original===
“Must I tell you, Lord?”

“Thou must,” said the King.

For a minute, Thorgnyr abode silent. Then, “This it is, then,” answered he: “that Styrbiorn’s stout stomach shall likely undo both he himself and us.”

“Pshaw!” said the King, “thou’rt jaundiced. Thou seest all yellow.”

“Say rather, Lord,” replied Thorgnyr, “that were a blind goose that knew not a fox from a fern-bush. At this Thing, ill as it was that it should have come to such a pass, I could have smoothed all, but by his row and ruckling was all upsy-turvy turned, and the Thing broke up in an uproar. He first set the ball flying, and returned ’em gibe for gibe and fierce word for fierce word.”

The King said menacingly, “They stoned my kinsman and my Earl, I am let to know.”

===Chinese===
“一定要告诉您么，主上？”

“一定要，”王说。

松格尼尔又沉默了一分钟。然后他答道：“那么便是这个：斯蒂尔比约恩那股子狂傲的心气，多半要毁了他自己，也毁了咱们。”

“呸！”王说，“你害了黄疸病了，看什么都是黄的。”

“不如说，主上，”松格尼尔答道，“是那只瞎了眼的鹅，分不清狐狸和蕨草丛。这次大会——事情闹到那步田地固然糟糕——我本可以全都抚平的，却被他一阵吵闹折腾得底朝天，大会在喧腾中散了场。头一个把球踢起来的就是他，人家一句挖苦他还一句挖苦，人家一句狠话他还一句狠话。”

王带着威胁说：“我可听说了，他们用石头砸了我的亲人、我的伯爵。”

===Original===
“I could not help it,” said Thorgnyr. And he paused. “Will you suffer me to speak plain, Lord?”

The King said, “Speak.”

“You have known the truth of my mind these forty year and more. And afore that, my father served the King your father, and counselled him faithfully with wholesome counsel. Truly I say unto you, King, the Swede-folk will not abear to be spur-ridden; and most unbearable shall be the spurrings of this young man.”

“And I,” said King Eric, swinging round on him in a flash of anger, “will not abear false kings i’ the land.”

“If you will take my rede, Lord,” said Thorgnyr, quailing not at all, “you will be so high-minded as, eaglelike, to disdain this little fowl. I swear to you I had no part in it, but stay them then I could not. ’Twas done in a hot folly of rage, under what stress of provocation you do know. It will die out like a spark, unless you, Lord, by untimely blowing on’t should puff it to flame indeed.”

“I,” said the King, “tread down sparks, not blow ’em, whenso I’ve a mind to put ’em out. The bonders know me, and I them. If I reach not out mine hand now upon this Lambi they will not mistake the reason; nor he neither, if he have wit. Bid him keep quiet, or safest depart out of the land. Thou hast ways and means: see to it, thou. Styrbiorn I will send abroad for three years, furnishing his depart with both ships and men sufficient for notable doings if, as I think, he hath the parts to use them. So shall he redden tooth somewhat otherwise than on mine own men here in Sweden, and after three years come home ripe to be received into kingdom.”

===Chinese===
“我拦不住，”松格尼尔说。他顿了顿。“您容我直言么，主上？”

王说：“讲。”

“这四十多年，您深知我心里的实情。再往前，我父亲侍奉您的父王，以良谋忠心辅佐他。我实实在在告诉您，王：瑞典的百姓忍受不了被人打着马刺骑；而这个年轻人抽的马刺，最叫人忍无可忍。”

“而我，”埃里克王说，怒气一闪，猛地转向他，“也忍受不了国中有僭王。”

“您若肯听我的主意，主上，”松格尼尔说，毫无畏缩之色，“便拿出宽宏的胸怀，像雄鹰一样，不屑理会这只小雀。我向您起誓我与此事无干，只是当时实在拦不住他们。那是一时狂怒之下的糊涂行径，受了怎样的挑衅，您是知道的。它自会像火星一样熄灭——除非您，主上，不合时宜地去吹它，当真把它吹成烈焰。”

“我么，”王说，“想灭火的时候，是踩灭火星，不是吹它。自由农们了解我，我也了解他们。此刻我若不朝这兰比伸手，他们不会误会缘故；他自己也不会，只要他有脑子。你叫兰比安静待着，或者最稳妥是离开这片国土。你有的是门路：这件事你去办。斯蒂尔比约恩，我要送他出海三年，给他配足船只人手，足够干几件大事——若他真如我所想有那份才具。这样，他的牙齿便到别处去见血，而不是咬我瑞典自己人；三年之后归来，正好成熟，可以入朝为王。”

===Original===
“You plant well, King,” said Thorgnyr, shaking his head. “Pray Gods the mould prove not barren and unapt.”

A man of the King’s bodyguard came in and louting before the King asked would he see Styrbiorn. The King bade admit him straight. Styrbiorn came swiftly in, saw Thorgnyr, and came to a full stop betwixt the carved jambs of the doorway. He looked from the King to Thorgnyr, from Thorgnyr to the King. His face grew grim and the hair of his head was raised a little, like the heckles of a savage dog in the presence of an enemy. “It is the worst of shames,” said he, “if you, Lord, will discourse thus friendly with that old man. Let lead him out and hew him before the doors; that were a good deed.”

The King gazed sternly at him, but spake no word.

“Lord,” said Styrbiorn, standing still in the doorway in the light of the fire, “I came to ask a boon of you. And this it is, that you find me twenty ships, Lord, and suffer me go a-harrying. And give me leave too to slay your outlaw Lambi the White, which these shagamuffin bonders and that old man did openly name for king. And that was the biggest shame ever heard tell of.”

Thorgnyr’s eyes were bent on the young man from under their deep-jutting eaves. His face was calm and his brow unruffled, but none might see the eyes of him watching from those dark sockets where the fitful firelight never pierced.

“Kinsman,” said King Eric, “thou art young. Therefore at thine injustice and want of judgment I wonder not: years and knowledge shall mend it. For the rest, not twenty but sixty ships shalt thou have, and a full tale of men thereto, and it is my will that thou be three winters abroad. There shalt thou, being king-born, learn the trade of kings. After that, if out of so many and great scapes thou come back safe and sound, I think I shall find thee a man grown, and a right son of thy father’s, and a right kinsman of mine. And that shall be the best day of all my life’s days, when I shall receive thee in thy father’s stead, joint King with me in Upsala.”

===Chinese===
“王，您栽种得好，”松格尼尔摇着头说，“只求上苍保佑，那块土别又贫瘠又不相宜。”

王的一名卫士进来，向王躬身请示：可愿意见斯蒂尔比约恩。王命即刻引他进来。斯蒂尔比约恩快步进来，一见松格尼尔，便在雕花的门柱之间猛然站定。他的目光从王移到松格尼尔，又从松格尼尔移到王。他的脸沉了下来，头发微微竖起，像恶狗见了仇人时颈毛倒竖。“真是奇耻大辱，”他说，“您竟肯同那老头这样亲热地说话。让人把他拉出去，剁死在门前：那才是一桩善举。”

王严厉地瞪着他，一言不发。

“主上，”斯蒂尔比约恩站在门口，映着火光，说道，“我来是求您一件事。就是请您拨我二十艘船，主上，准我出海劫掠。还求您准我杀了您那被放逐的白人兰比——那班蓬头垢面的自由农和那老头子，公然立了他为王。那是亘古未有的奇耻大辱。”

松格尼尔深眉如檐，目光自檐下落在那年轻人身上。他面色平静，眉头舒展，但谁也看不见他的眼睛——那双眼睛在黑暗的眼窝深处注视着，忽明忽灭的火光从未照进去过。

“亲人，”埃里克王说，“你年纪轻。所以你的不公与见识短浅，我并不奇怪：年岁与学识自会补足它。至于别的——不是二十艘，是六十艘船给你，配满额的人手；而且我要你在海外过三个冬天。在那里，你有王者血统，正好学一学为王的手艺。之后，你若能从那么多、那么大的凶险里安然无恙地回来，我想我见到的将是一个长成了的男人，你父亲真正的儿子，我真正的亲人。那将是我一生之中最好的一天：我接你入你父亲之位，与我在乌普萨拉共为王。”

===Original===
When the King his uncle had so spoken, Styrbiorn’s face put off in an instant his fierce and dogged look and he looked upon the King with so open and merry a smile as must have made even an ill-willer wish to love him, and his hair bristled no more but lay down as it should upon his head. He came forward into the room.

“As for this Lambi,” said the King, “he is naught: a fly: a gob of spittle: I regard him not at all. I forbid thee, on thy life, to fight with him in Sweden or the coasts thereof. But if ye hap together in the outlands or on the main seas, why, let it befall as Fate shall will.”

“King,” answered Styrbiorn, “you have nobly dealt with me. And I swear to abide by all you bid me.”

The King said, “I will now that ye twain be friends, thou and Thorgnyr. I will you to handsel friendship to each other, here in this place.”

Thorgnyr held out his hand; but Styrbiorn paused, then stepped a pace backward. “I’ll give him my hand,” he said, “when I can give it with a good will. Another day.”

===Chinese===
他舅舅王说完这话，斯蒂尔比约恩脸上的凶悍执拗霎时褪尽，望向王的笑容那么开朗、那么欢喜，纵是心存恶意的人见了也要想爱他；他的头发不再竖着，服帖地伏在头上。他走进房里来。

“至于这个兰比，”王说，“他什么也不是：一只苍蝇：一口唾沫：我全然不把他放在心上。我严禁你——拿你的命担保——在瑞典或其沿海与他交手。但你们若在外邦或大海上撞见了，那便听凭命运安排吧。”

“王，”斯蒂尔比约恩答道，“你待我如此高义。我起誓，凡你吩咐的，我一切都遵从。”

王说：“我现在要你们两个做朋友，你和松格尼尔。我要你们在此地，互赠信物，结为友好。”

松格尼尔伸出手来；斯蒂尔比约恩却顿了顿，退后一步。“等我能够心甘情愿的时候，”他说，“我自会把我的手给他。改日吧。”

===Original===
“Well,” said Thorgnyr, “at least I love open honesty.”

“The lad hath been sore tried,” said the King when Styrbiorn was gone. “I do see greatness in him.”

Thorgnyr looked at the King from the inscrutable dark of his deep-thatched eye-sockets. “Ay, Lord,” he said. “But the end tries all.”

===Chinese===
“好吧，”松格尼尔说，“至少我喜爱这份坦荡的诚实。”

“这孩子受了太多磨砺，”斯蒂尔比约恩走后，王说，“我确实在他身上看到了伟大。”

松格尼尔从浓眉覆蔽、深不可测的眼窝的黑暗中望着王。“是啊，主上，”他说，“但结局检验一切。”

## III / 三

===Original===
Queen Sigrid the Haughty

Styrbiorn now fared abroad according to the King’s command. And summer wore, and winter, and when winter was well past King Eric came south to Arland to guest with Skogul-Tosti, the father of that Sigrid who was with Styrbiorn on King Olaf’s howe, and saw visions there as is aforesaid. Tosti was a great friend of the King’s, and made him noble entertainment; and when the King had sat there three days with his folk that were with him Tosti prayed him sit another three, and when those were done he prayed him yet three days more, so that nine days all told they feasted it in Tosti’s hall.

Skogul-Tosti was a portly man and a stately, and the most open handed of men in all things which belong to housekeeping, and a showy man in his dress. It liked him better that his worthiness should call him to that reputation, to be the greatest man in all the countryside he might overlook from his home-mead at Hawkby, rather than he should exercise larger dominion over lands and folk and yet be called but the King’s man, and the tool of a greatness not his own. He was the greatest of warriors, and spent much time a-warring. His wife was named Gudrid. She was a stirring woman, of the kin of the Earls of West Gautland. She was fair to look on, but folk deemed her over proud and grasping and somewhat hard of heart.

At this ninth night of their feast, when men had well eaten and were fallen now to jesting and story-telling and drinking one to another, the King spake and said, “Early it is, and yet, Tosti, I will drink no more, except thy daughter Sigrid bear cup to me.”

Sigrid was set on the cross-bench on the dais, beside her mother, and the other womenfolk that were at the feast sat out away from them on either hand. She had a gown of blue, collared and purfled with mink’s fur, and about the neck of it sets of gold, in every set four pieces of amber. And she had above her brow a fillet of silk and twisted wire of gold, to keep back from her face the red deep masses of her hair, that came down in two thick and heavy trammels or plaits beside her bosom on either hand, and the ends tucked up under her jewelled belt.

===Chinese===
高傲的王后西格丽德

斯蒂尔比约恩于是遵王之命出海去了。夏天过去了，冬天也过去了；冬意阑珊之时，埃里克王南下来到阿兰德，在斯科古尔-托斯蒂家做客——托斯蒂便是那位西格丽德的父亲；前文说过，她曾在奥拉夫王的坟冢上与斯蒂尔比约恩同处，并在那里见了幻象。托斯蒂是王的老友，待他以隆重的款待；王与随从在他家坐了三天，托斯蒂请他再坐三天；三天又毕，又请再坐三天——如此前后九天，他们在托斯蒂的厅堂里饮宴。

斯科古尔-托斯蒂身材魁伟，仪态庄重，凡属家计门户之事，是天下最慷慨好客的人，穿着又讲究华美。他宁愿凭自己的功德赢得那样的名声——做霍克比家园草场上放眼所及整个乡野中最伟大的人物——而不愿统辖更多的土地人民，却只落得被人唤作王的臣属、他人伟业的工具。他是头等的战士，大半光阴都在征战里度过。他的妻子名叫古德丽德，是个精明强干的女人，出身西耶特兰伯爵的亲族。她容貌姣好，但人们嫌她过于高傲贪求，心肠也有些硬。

宴至第九夜，众人已酒足饭饱，正彼此打趣、说古、互相劝饮。王开口道：“时候还早呢，可是托斯蒂，若不是令嫒西格丽德为我捧杯，我便不再饮了。”

西格丽德坐在高台上横置的女眷席上，挨着她的母亲；赴宴的其余女眷分坐两侧，离她们稍远。她穿一件蓝色长裙，领口缘边镶着貂皮，颈间围着几组金饰，每组缀着四块琥珀。她额上束着一条丝带，带着绞金丝的绦子，把她深红色的浓发从脸上拢向脑后；头发编成两条粗重的发辫，垂在胸前两侧，辫梢掖在缀宝的腰带底下。

===Original===
Tosti, from his high-seat on the lower bench, called her by name and said, “Why dally, Sigrid? Are we become so slack as keep our guests waiting? and much more when ’tis the King.”

She, glancing with her dark eyes first in her father’s face then in the King’s, reached out her hand now to the great golden drinking horn which a thrall, obedient to her father’s nod, proffered to her full of mead so that the foam of it ran down the sides. As a ship’s mast, half laid over by a gust of wind, rises erect from the trough of the wave, so rose she, and with modest and downward look came past the benches till she was stood beside the King.

King Eric took the horn with one hand and the lady with the other, and made her sit beside him in the high seat. From this she at first hung aback, but he would have his way. She sat here very demure, answering but ay or no or with a flickering smile to whatso the King might say to her: very quiet and estranged. So, and in such a quietness, Sigrid sat in the high seat beside the King; till the fires burned low, and men’s eyelids waxed heavy, and it was late night, and the feast was done.

===Chinese===
托斯蒂从下席的高座上唤她的名字，说：“为何磨蹭，西格丽德？咱们家几时这样怠慢，叫客人干等了？何况客人还是王。”

她那双黑眼睛先扫过父亲的脸，又扫过王的脸，伸手去接那只大金角杯——一个奴仆应着她父亲的颔首，把满斟的蜜酒捧到她面前，酒沫顺着杯壁流下来。如同一根被狂风压弯的船桅从浪谷里重新直起，她这样站起身来，垂着眼，端庄娴静地从条凳间走过来，直到立在王的身旁。

埃里克王一手接过角杯，一手挽住那位小姐，让她在高座上坐在自己身边。她起初推却，王却非要如此。她坐在那里，十分矜持，王对她无论说什么，她只答个是或不是，或是浅浅一笑：安静而疏远。就这样，西格丽德安安静静地坐在王身旁的高座上，直到炉火渐低，众人眼皮渐沉，夜已深沉，宴席方散。

===Original===
---

“This is what thou hast long set thy mind on,” said Skogul-Tosti to his wife at his coming to bed, “and I think thou mayst be glad at this night’s work.”

“So far, good,” said she. “But the ship’s not beached yet.”

“Not beached yet? Beached it is, and laid up,” said Tosti. “Thou shalt see, ere the King ride hence tomorrow he will bespeak her in marriage for Styrbiorn.”

“That will be well so far,” said she.

===Chinese===
---

“这正是你长久以来打的算盘，”斯科古尔-托斯蒂上床就寝时对妻子说，“我想，今晚这一遭，你该高兴了。”

“到这一步，还好，”她说，“可船还没靠岸呢。”

“还没靠岸？靠了岸了，还上了排了，”托斯蒂说，“你瞧着吧，明日王还没骑马走，就要替斯蒂尔比约恩向她求婚了。”

“那样的话，倒还好，”她说。

===Original===
“Well? what better?” Tosti, that was sat taking off his shoes, stared wonderingly up in his wife’s smiling and doubting face. “And by thy good schemings he and she have been good friends together too.”

“She hath said no to a dozen ere now, and not one of them but had been a great match such as should do us honour. Hast forgot how thy young messmate fared, Harald the Grenlander? and he is a king now.”

“True enough,” said he. “The lass hath a stubborn will, that is true. And her very scornfulness and haughty ways seem to draw ’em on, only to send ’em packing. But there’s bigger game on the wing here.”

“That is true, too,” said Gudrid. “But, remember: thou’st a wayward daughter.”

“A right daughter of thine, mistress. As skittish as an eel.” Gudrid laughed. “But she hath wit,” he said: “she can lick a dish before a cat. She’ll ne’er say nay to Styrbiorn.”

“I say only this,” said Gudrid: “be not too certain sure.”

===Chinese===
“还好？还能比这更好？”托斯蒂正坐着脱鞋，诧异地仰头看妻子那张含笑又存疑的脸。“再说，亏得你安排得好，他跟她也早就相熟相好了。”

“到如今她已回绝过一打人了，那里面随便哪一个，都是能给咱们挣体面的好亲事。你忘了你那位年轻伙伴的下场么，那个格伦兰人哈拉尔？他如今可是王。”

“倒也是，”他说，“这丫头主意犟，不假。她那股子轻慢和高傲劲儿，偏偏招得他们前仆后继，只为挨她一顿排揎。可这一回飞的是只大得多的鸟。”

“这话也不错，”古德丽德说，“可是，记住：你有个任性的女儿。”

“真真是你的亲闺女，夫人。滑得像条泥鳅。”古德丽德笑了。“可她有脑子，”他说：“猫还没到，她先把碟子舔干净了。她绝不会对斯蒂尔比约恩说不。”

“我只说一句，”古德丽德道：“别把话说得太满。”

===Original===
“ ’Tis a wonder past guessing,” said Tosti, standing up, “all these doubts and questions of thine. Hast spoke to her on’t?”

“No,” said she. “But I watched her. She smelt well enough what was toward tonight. I liked not the face she put on it.”

“But not say no to this? There’s no higher game to fly at.”

Gudrid shook her head. “ ’Tis thy giving of her her will too much, and cockering of her. She’d ne’er mind me, and now not thee neither.”

“Well,” said Tosti, “she shall have her will, too, whatsome’er she choose. Albeit, I’ll ne’er believe she’d say nay to this.”

Gudrid said nothing, but stood looking at him as on some new and entertaining thing. He, knowing not what to make of her and her looks, took her by the shoulders. “She hath this of thee,” he said, “that she is like to be an ill curse to any man save the man of her own choosing.” And he drew his wife to him and kissed her on the neck and bare shoulder.

===Chinese===
“真是猜不透的怪事，”托斯蒂站起身来，“你这些疑神疑鬼。你跟她提过这事么？”

“没有，”她说，“可我留意她了。今晚要出什么事，她闻得清清楚楚。我不喜欢她摆出的那副脸色。”

“可这一回她还能说不？再没有更高贵的人家可攀了。”

古德丽德摇头。“都怪你太由着她的性子，把她娇惯坏了。她从不听我的，如今连你的也不听了。”

“好吧，”托斯蒂说，“这一回也由她，随她挑什么。不过我才不信，这样的事她会说不。”

古德丽德不说话，只站在那里打量他，像看一件新鲜有趣的物事。他摸不透她、也摸不透她的神色，便抓住她的双肩。“她这一点随了你，”他说，“这辈子怕是要祸害天下男人，除了她自己挑中的那一个。”说着把妻子拉近，吻她的脖颈和裸露的肩头。

===Original===
Next morning the King took Tosti apart and said to him, “There is a matter I have to move unto thee, Tosti, and I think after last night it will not take thee napping. And it toucheth thy daughter Sigrid.”

Tosti answered, “Your drift, Lord, is not hidden from me, and I do embrace it for the greatest honour and gladness that ever did or could befall me and my kindred. Yet since true is that which is said, ‘There’s many a thing in the carle’s cot that is not in the king’s garth,’ and since this is mine only daughter, I know you will not take it ill, Lord, if I leave unto her the choosing in this. So have I alway done heretofore, and so it seemeth me will be best now, both for her and for all that have part herein.”

“I will talk to her myself,” said the King. “None did yet die of another’s wound, nor should any be content with another’s choosing.”

The King walked with Sigrid forth beyond the home-mead by sheep-ways on to the open fell-sides south toward Balingsdale. For a long while he was silent. Then suddenly he said, “I have a suit unto thee, Sigrid.”

“That is not hard to guess, Lord,” said she, dreaming not that the King had any other purpose than this, whereof she had long since had an inkling in her parents’ minds, to wed her and Styrbiorn.

===Chinese===
次日清晨，王把托斯蒂拉到一旁，对他说：“托斯蒂，我有一件事要向你提。我想经过昨夜，你也不会措手不及。这事关你的女儿西格丽德。”

托斯蒂答道：“您的来意，主上，我已明白；我满心欢喜地接受，这是我和我全族所曾得到、或可能得到的最大荣耀。只是老话说得好：‘农户的茅屋里，也藏着王家院子里没有的物件。’何况她是我唯一的女儿。主上，若我把选择之权留给她本人，想您不会见怪。我素来如此待她；如今看来，这样于她、于凡与此事相干的人，都最好。”

“我自己去同她讲，”王说，“没人会死于别人的伤口，也没人该满足于别人的选择。”

王与西格丽德一道散步，出了家园的草场，沿羊肠小径走上南面通往巴林斯谷的开阔山坡。他沉默了好一阵，忽然说：“西格丽德，我有一件事求你。”

“这不难猜到，主上，”她说，万万想不到王除了这门亲事还会有别的意图——把她许配给斯蒂尔比约恩这件事，她早就从父母的心思里嗅出些端倪了。

===Original===
“What answer,” said the King, “must I have of thee?”

Sigrid, looking straight before her, replied, “I will tell you, Lord, if you will tell me first whether it resteth in my free choice to say yes or no to you.”

“Thy father,” said King Eric, “hath laid all in thine hand, to answer as thou shalt please. Besides, I would take no other answer.”

“Then, Lord,” said she, “my answer is No.”

At that, the King stopped short. Sigrid too stopped and faced him. Her face was very red. “I must have thy reason,” said the King.

===Chinese===
“那我必须从你这里得到怎样的回答？”王说。

西格丽德目视前方，答道：“您若先告诉我，对您说是或说不，是否全凭我的自由选择，我便告诉您。”

“你父亲，”埃里克王说，“已把一切交到你手里，随你高兴怎样回答。再说，别的回答我也不要。”

“那么，主上，”她说，“我的回答是不。”

王闻言站住。西格丽德也停下，转过身来面对他。她的脸通红。“我必须听听你的理由。”王说。

===Original===
She made no answer.

The King said, “I will put no stress on thee, Sigrid. But as well as I am minded to entreat thee, surely so well it befitteth thee to answer me and tell me thy reasons. For this match is not as another, which haply thy high mind might not think honourable enough for thee.”

Still for a minute she faced the King in silence. Very proud she looked, her brown eyes deep and unsearchable like a deer’s; then her eyes dropped and she turned away. “I see,” said the King: “this hath come over sudden upon thee.”

Sigrid laughed. “Nay, Lord, ’tis but the old tale again, the old song.” And she began to say:

===Chinese===
她不作答。

王说：“我不用强逼你，西格丽德。但我既以礼相待，你便也该以礼答复我，把你的理由告诉我。这门亲事非同寻常，不是那种你高傲的心或许觉得配不上你的寻常姻缘。”

她又默默面对了王一分钟，神色极为倨傲，棕色的眼睛深邃莫测，像鹿的眼睛；随后她垂下眼帘，转过脸去。“我明白了，”王说：“这事对你来得太突然。”

西格丽德笑了。“不，主上，这不过是老故事又来一遍，老歌又唱一遍。”于是她念道：

===Original===
“I ken a verse:

					An eagle sat upon a stane!

					And I ken another:

					An eagle sat upon a stane!

===Chinese===
我知道一支歌谣：

					一只鹰蹲在石头上！

					我还知道另一支：

					一只鹰蹲在石头上！

===Original===
					And I ken a third:

					An eagle sat upon a stane!

					The first is like ’em all:

					An eagle sat upon a stane!”

===Chinese===
					我还知道第三支：

					一只鹰蹲在石头上！

					第一支跟它们全都一个样：

					一只鹰蹲在石头上！

===Original===
She looked up at him, an angry, mocking look. The King’s face hardened. She threw out her hands, and, “What can a maid do withal,” said she, “if men do so plague her? Must I take this untried boy because he is come of kingly blood? and because he hath the King to come a-wooing for him? And truly,” she said, turning her head away, “I do hold Styrbiorn no better than a cat’s son.”

King Eric stared: then, seeing which way the wind set, he brake out a-laughing and reached out and took her by the hand. “Why, here’s a pretty diversion,” said he, “of cross questions and crooked answers. I will woo thee, Sigrid, for no man’s hand but mine own. And this is my suit to thee, to be mine own wedded wife, and Queen in Upsala.”

Her hand yet in the King’s hand, her body poised in free proud lines like the wild birches, daughters of the fell and the free elements, Sigrid stood very still. Her breathing was quickened. After a while the King said, “And now, what answer wilt thou give me?”

They had halted by the margin of a tarn, part thawed. The wind blowing from the far side drove the ice up on to the bank where they stood. The packing ice crackled and moaned with a soft and high-pitched moaning, and the broken bits of it tinkled in the wind-stirred water with a tinkling as of faint bells. Sigrid said, very low, “That was one answer I gave you, when I thought you did speak for Styrbiorn.” Then, starting suddenly out of her quiet, she strove to withdraw her hand from the King’s, but he kept it. “I pray you let me go,” she cried. “You will bruise my hand.” Then her face flamed red and her eyes turned hard and fierce: “Must I answer you, Lord, as to the King? or as to an old man come a-wooing?”

The King, letting go her hand indeed, caught her in his arms. She, frightened as soon as she had spoken it lest her gibe, that she could not bite in, of old man, might yet have lost her that high advancement which her soul lusted for, abode breathless in his strong embrace that taught her how empty was her gibe, and, being empty, harmless. “I will show thee,” said King Eric, speaking hot and close in her ear and hair, “how thou must answer me. Thus would I have thee answer me, Sigrid, as to a great man of war that do love thee, and will not let thee go. And will not let thee go: Sigrid the Haughty.”

Yet, so hard and stiff abode she in his embrace, without word spoken, that he at length did let her go. She stood back now with hands behind her, facing him steadily with those liquid eyes inscrutable. “It is well seen, Lord,” said she, “that you are stronger than I. Yet remember, there be two needed for every agreement.”

===Chinese===
她抬眼看他，目光又恼又嘲。王的脸沉了下来。她摊开双手，说道：“男人们这样纠缠一个姑娘，叫她有什么法子？就因为他生在王家，我就得收下这个没经过世面的毛头小子？就因为国王亲自替他来做媒？说实话，”她说着把头扭开，“在我眼里，斯蒂尔比约恩不过是猫崽子一只。”

埃里克王怔住了：随即看出风往哪边吹，放声大笑，伸手握住她的手。“哈，好一出妙戏，”他说，“问得拐弯抹角，答得七歪八扭。西格丽德，我这回求婚，不为任何人的手，只为我自己的手。我求你的就是这样：做我明媒正娶的妻子，做乌普萨拉的王后。”

她的手还在王的手里，身姿亭亭，自由而高傲，如荒山的野桦——那是山岭与自由风露的女儿。西格丽德静静地站着。她的呼吸急促起来。过了一会儿，王说：“那么现在，你要给我怎样的回答？”

他们停在一片小湖边，湖面化开了一半。风从对岸吹来，把冰推挤到他们站的岸边。挤叠的冰噼啪作响，发出尖细而柔和的哀鸣；碎冰在风搅动的水里叮当作响，如隐约的铃音。西格丽德极低地说：“方才我以为您是替斯蒂尔比约恩开口，那便算我给过您一个回答了。”随即她猛然从静默中惊起，使劲要把手从王手里抽回，王却握住不放。“求您放开我，”她喊道，“您要把我的手捏伤了。”她的脸腾地红了，眼神变得又硬又凶：“主上，我该把您当王来回答呢，还是当一个前来求亲的老头儿？”

王果然放开了她的手，却把她搂进怀里。她方才那句收不回来的“老头儿”的挖苦话一出口就害了怕，惟恐因此失掉她灵魂渴慕的那场高攀；她屏息不动，待在他有力的怀抱里——那怀抱告诉她，她的挖苦多么空洞，空洞，便也无害。“我来教你，”埃里克王贴着她的耳朵和头发，热烈地低语，“你该怎样回答我。我要你这样回答我，西格丽德：把我当作一个爱你、绝不肯放你走的战争伟人。绝不肯放你走：高傲的西格丽德。”

然而她在怀抱中那样僵直地硬撑着，一言不发，他终究还是放开了她。她退后站着，双手背在身后，用那双水汪汪的、深不可测的眼睛静静地直视他。“看得很明白，主上，”她说，“您比我强壮。可是请记住：任何盟约，须得两个人肯。”

===Original===
“That is but fair and right,” said the King. “There shall no constraint be put upon thee, whether of hand or word. But here in this place shalt thou choose.”

She stood silent.

“Thou shalt have till tomorrow morn, then,” said the King.

But still she stood silent. May be, strange and contrarious thoughts contended within her head, under the braided heaviness of tresses that flung back the weak sun’s radiance in shifting flashes of red-gold fire. At length she turned full on the King those brown eyes of hers: “And which way would you have me choose, Lord?” said she.

“Which way?” said the King. “That is a strange question, and needeth no answer.”

Again for a while she was silent. Her eyes were troublous to a man to behold when they looked so upon him; even as a man might voyage over an undiscovered sea, uncertain whether these were safe unplumbed depths he sailed over, or but shallow rock-strewn waters apt for his destruction. Then suddenly her eyelids flickered and her proud lips sweetened and she became all yielding loveliness. “The strong man’s rede ruleth still,” she said, and came to his arms.

===Chinese===
“这是公道，”王说，“无论手上还是口上，我都不强迫你。但就在此地，你要做出选择。”

她默默站着。

“那么，给你到明天早晨。”王说。

她依然默默站着。在那编成辫的厚重发髻下面——那发把淡弱的日光化作流转的红金色火光明晃晃地掷回去——也许正有奇异而悖逆的念头在彼此角斗。末了她把那双棕色的眼睛完完全全转向王：“那您要我往哪边选呢，主上？”她说。

“往哪边？”王说，“这个问题奇怪，不必回答。”

她又沉默了一会儿。她的眼睛这样望着一个男人时，那目光令人心乱；如同航行在一片未经测绘的海上，拿不准帆底下是安全的无底深渊，还是遍布暗礁的浅滩，一招覆舟之祸。忽然，她的眼睫颤动，倨傲的嘴唇变得温柔，整个人化作了柔情百转的妩媚。“仍是强者的主意做主，”她说着，投入了他的怀抱。

## IV / 四

===Original===
Jomsburg

Palnatoki of Fion dwelt in those days in Jomsburg, and was captain there. A marvellous sure place had he made it, builded with stone walls about and about, and he had, in the gut of the harbour-mouth, a sea-gate like to the gate of a walled town, so as a three hundred long-ships might enter and ride in the harbour within the sea-gate. And in the castle or burg was good houseroom for every man of them that was in the Lay (or, as some say, the Law) of the Jomsburgers, and treasure chambers enow to keep safe the good treasure they brought home thither from their harrying, and store chambers filled with corn and meal and kippered fish and all manner of victual and stores. And there was within that burg a well of fresh water always flowing, and both landward and seaward it was a place not to be fought against, girt about with thick high walls inexpugnable and stone cliffs washed with the sea.

There was as then with Palnatoki in Jomsburg these lords, to wit: Bui the Thick of Borgundholm and Sigurd Cape, his brother; and Biorn the Brisk; and those three sons of Strut-Harald that was in that time a famous Earl in Skaney, Sigvaldi namely and Heming and Thorkell the High; and these three were brothers-in-law to Bui and Sigurd, since Sigurd had to wife Tofa the daughter of Strut-Harald. Besides these there were in Jomsburg many lords and men of mark, and all in their prime age and with power and means to hand with their will; blood-suppers, every one ready to leap, like a hand-wolf, into his natural wildness: and yet (which indeed made the main strength and anchor of their state) every one ready to leave, in all things of matter, the whole regiment of that burg and host with Palnatoki.

For every man of them, were he of great estate or were he least among their fellowship, must swear a strong oath to hold by the lay of the Jomsburgers. And therein were these things covenanted:

===Chinese===
约姆斯堡

菲翁的帕尔纳托基那时住在约姆斯堡，为堡中之首。他把这地方经营得固若金汤：四面八方砌起石墙；又在港门的咽喉处设了一道海闸，如同设防城池的城门一般，三百艘长船可以由此鱼贯而入，停泊在闸内的港中。堡寨里为每个遵约姆斯堡盟法（“Lay”，或如有些人所说，“Law”）的汉子都备有宽裕的居处；有足够的藏宝库，妥藏他们每次劫掠归来的好财物；还有满仓的谷物、面粉、腌鱼和各色粮秣军需。堡中有一口长流不涸的淡水井；无论陆路海路，这里都是不可与争之地：厚墙高垒环抱四周，坚不可拔，石崖壁立，海浪冲刷。

其时与帕尔纳托基同在约姆斯堡的贵公有：博格恩霍尔姆的壮汉布伊及其弟西古尔德·卡帕；捷足比约恩；还有斯特鲁特-哈拉尔的三个儿子——那斯特鲁特-哈拉尔其时是斯卡涅有名的伯爵——即西格瓦迪、海明与高个托克尔；这三人与布伊和西古尔德是姻亲，因为西古尔德娶了斯特鲁特-哈拉尔的女儿托法。除他们之外，约姆斯堡中还有许多豪杰名流，个个正当盛年，权柄财力俱在掌握，随心所欲；个个是啖血之徒，随时可以像一头豢熟的狼，一跃而返于天生的野性：然而（这也正是他们这份基业的主要力量与锚定之处）凡属大事，个个都甘愿把堡寨与队伍的统辖之权，尽付帕尔纳托基一人。

因为他们中间每一个人，无论地位多高，或是在盟中身份最低微，都必须立下重誓，恪守约姆斯堡人的盟法。盟法中立约的条款如下：

===Original===
First, that there should no man be received in that fellowship older than fifty nor any younger than eighteen winters. Nor yet any that would run from another of like strength and like-weaponed with himself. And thirdly, that every man of them should be sworn to avenge another’s slaying, as though he were his own brother. Fourthly, that none should kindle slander among men there. Fifth, though they should take to a man which had slain father or brother of one that was already there before him, or a kinsman of his, if that came to light after their taking to him, then should the whole award and settlement of this lie with Palnatoki. Also, whatsoever of tidings might thither come, none should be so hardy as tell aught thereof, seeing that it was for Palnatoki alone to tell all tidings. And he that should be found in default touching aught of these things aforesaid, the same should be driven away and turned out of their law. None might have a woman in the burg. Nor none might be abroad longer than three nights but by leave of Palnatoki. Whatsoever they might get with harrying they must bear it to the pole, were it much or little, so it were money’s worth; and if it were shown of any man that he did not so, then must that man be put away and out of their law, were it much or little. No man might speak or utter there any craven word, howsoever hopeless a turn their affair might take. There could not anything befall within the burg of such a kind as that they themselves must shape and settle it, but Palnatoki alone must there rule all things according to his will. Nor might ties of kinship nor of friendship sway the matter if men should wish to come thither who were not in their law; no, not though at the bidding of men already there they should come thither, it no whit availed them if they were not fit.

So they sat in the burg in good peace, and kept well their laws. They fared abroad every summer a-harrying in this land and in that and gat them good fee and good renown. And they were held for the greatest champions and men of war, and scarcely was any man thought their match in that time.

Now was summer-day long past of that summer following the things in Sweden we have told of, and they of Jomsburg were home from the sea to discharge their havings and fit their ships anew, and so fare forth again one more voyage afore winter. Palnatoki, walking betimes on the seawall facing the brow of dawn, beheld ships bear down out of the main northeastward under oar and sail: sixty, steering for Jomsburg. There was a land-breeze blowing, light and fitful, and the sea was smooth under Jomsburg walls, with shags fishing and diving and a whirl of white sea-mews screaming and gaffling in the air above them. The ships drew near, till they were come within earshot. And by then were the most of the Jomsburgers stood on the seawall with Palnatoki.

The greatest of those ships drew in nearest under the wall. She was swart-black and fair with gold, and her sail was striped red and blue and green. Her prow was a drake’s head scaled with black iron and scarlet-tongued and with eyes and crest of shining gold, and the shields that hung along her gunwale from bow to stern were painted with many colours and studded with bronze and iron that blinked in the growing light of morning.

There stood on her poop a man in a blue kirtle and a ring-byrny of iron, and helmed with an iron helm burnished and inlaid with gold; and his helm was winged with the wings of a buzzard erect and shadowing above either ear. So when his ship was come over against that place whereas those lords of Jomsburg were stood together on the wall, that man uttered command and the oarsmen backed and held her. And he hailed them on the wall.

===Chinese===
第一，年过五十者不得收入那结义，不满十八冬者亦不得收入。第二，凡遇与自身力气相当、兵器相类之人对阵而逃跑者，不得收入。第三，人人须立誓为他人之死复仇，如同为自己的亲兄弟复仇一般。第四，任何人不得在盟中挑拨是非诽谤。第五，他们固然可以接纳一个杀过某位先入盟者之父、之兄或亲族的人；但此事若在接纳之后才败露，则全案的裁决与处置，皆归帕尔纳托基一人。又，无论有何等消息传到堡中，谁也不得胆敢外传分毫，因为一切消息只许帕尔纳托基一人发布。凡被查出违背上述任何一款者，即行驱逐，革出盟法之外。堡中不得留妇人。未经帕尔纳托基准许，任何人不得在外过三夜以上。劫掠所得，无论多寡，只要是有价之物，必须尽数搬到柱前；若查出有谁私匿不缴，无论多寡，即将此人逐出，革出盟法之外。无论局势落到何等绝望的地步，堡中任何人不得口出懦言。堡中无论发生何事，一概不许他们自行裁断处置，必须由帕尔纳托基一人依其意志裁决一切。凡未入盟法之人欲来相投，无论亲谊交情，皆不得徇私收留；纵然是应堡中旧人之邀而来，若其人不合格，也是徒然无用。

他们就这样在堡中安然度日，严守他们的法度。每年夏天出海劫掠，或到此国或到彼国，赢得了厚利与赫赫声名。世人推他们为天下第一等的勇士与战士，那时几乎无人被认为是他们的对手。

我们前面讲过瑞典那些事之后的那个夏天，夏至日早已过去，约姆斯堡人从海上归来，清点货物，重新整备船只，预备赶在入冬前再出航一次。帕尔纳托基清早在海堤上散步，面朝着黎明的天色，望见外海上东北方向有船队破浪而来，桨帆并用：整整六十艘，直指约姆斯堡。陆风轻拂，时断时续，约姆斯堡墙下的海面平滑如镜，鸬鹚在水里捕鱼潜水，一群白海鸥在它们上空盘旋尖叫。船队渐渐驶近，直到相闻可及。那时，大多数约姆斯堡人已与帕尔纳托基一同立在海堤上。

那些船中最大的一艘驶到墙根最近处。它通体乌黑，饰金华美，帆上红蓝绿条纹相间。船首是一颗龙首，披着黑铁鳞甲，吐着猩红的舌，眼与冠是灿亮的黄金；从船头到船尾悬在舷侧的一排盾牌彩绘斑斓，缀着青铜与铁饰，在渐亮的晨光里明灭闪烁。

船尾立着一个人，身穿蓝色长衣、铁环锁子甲，头戴磨亮的铁盔，盔上嵌金；盔顶双翼如鹞鹰振翅，直竖在两耳上方，翼影覆落。船驶到约姆斯堡众贵公聚集的那段堤前，正对面时，那人一声令下，桨手们倒桨停船。他向堤上的人招呼。

===Original===
Palnatoki gave back his greeting, and asked what man was he, and what countryman.

Then answered he from the poop, “I am Styrbiorn, whom men call the Swedes’ Champion, son of Olaf the Swede-King. And this is my host, and we be of the Swede-realm.”

Palnatoki asked what was their errand.

Styrbiorn answered, they would have guesting there.

“This is no guesthouse of Jomsburg,” answered Palnatoki. “And I and my mates be no lovers of guests nor guestings.”

Styrbiorn said, “To him of you that is Toki Palni’s son would I talk: to thee, with the nose like an eagle; or art not thou he?”

===Chinese===
帕尔纳托基回礼致意，问他是什么人，哪国之人。

那人从船尾答道：“我是斯蒂尔比约恩，人称瑞典人之勇士，瑞典人奥拉夫王之子。这是我的队伍，我们都是瑞典国的人。”

帕尔纳托基问他们来干什么。

斯蒂尔比约恩答道，他们想在那里做客。

“约姆斯堡不是客店，”帕尔纳托基答道，“我与我的伙伴们也不喜欢宾客与宴请。”

斯蒂尔比约恩说：“我想同你们中间那位托基·帕尔尼之子说话：就是你这鹰钩鼻的；莫非你不是他么？”

===Original===
Palnatoki answered, “I am that man.”

“Ay, and ’tis a bird hath claws,” cried Bui.

“We be not come hither a-begging,” said Styrbiorn to Palnatoki. “Neither art thou and thine wont to beg whether fee or forbearance at other men’s hands, nor yet the more am I and mine. This is mine errand: to offer you my friendship, and be with you in Jomsburg, and sail with you a-viking.”

“He stuttereth,” said Bui, “like a pig at the trough.”

Palnatoki gazed at Styrbiorn a minute narrowly, as a skipper studieth a ship or a horseman a horse. Then said he, “We need no man’s friendship, nor no help of thine. I have heard tell of thee. Thou art not yet grown to man’s estate. What can’st thou do?”

“I have harried two summers eastaway,” answered Styrbiorn. “And afore that, I slew mine uncle’s outlaw, Lambi the White, that was a sea-king of great note. That was off Skaney-side last summer.”

===Chinese===
帕尔纳托基答：“我便是那人。”

“是啊，老鹰可是有利爪的，”布伊喊道。

“我们不是来乞讨的，”斯蒂尔比约恩对帕尔纳托基说，“你和你的伙伴们素来不向人乞求财货或宽容，我和我的人更不会。我的来意是：向你们献上我的友谊，与你们同在约姆斯堡，与你们同去海上做维京人。”

“他说话结巴，”布伊说，“像食槽边的猪。”

帕尔纳托基把斯蒂尔比约恩细细打量了一分钟，像船长端详一条船，骑手端详一匹马。然后他说：“我们不需要谁的友谊，也不需要你的帮手。你的名声我听说过。你还没长成人。你能干什么？”

“我已在东边劫掠了两个夏天，”斯蒂尔比约恩答道，“在那之前，我杀了我舅舅那被放逐的白人兰比——他是个大有名头的海上之王。那是去年夏天，在斯卡涅海外。”

===Original===
Palnatoki asked whence they were come now.

He answered, “From guesting east in Holmgarth. And we harried summer-long in Biarmaland and Balagarth-Side. And not bootless came we thence.”

“If hence thou sailest, I think thou’lt leave that booty here,” said Bui.

Sigvaldi spake: “The ships be manned, Toki. ’Twere well an we rowed out forthright: take these conies, ere they take fright and up scut and away.”

Earl Wolf, that was by Styrbiorn on the poop, spake in his ear: “This is a rash folly, as I told thee. Seest not how they do lay their noses together in deliberation what to do? And ’tis not for our comfort nor furtherance.”

“Let be,” said Styrbiorn. And he called out on high to Palnatoki, “Take to us: you shall not find that in seeking friends you found table-guts to eat you and your meat. And you lack a man, I am for you.”

===Chinese===
帕尔纳托基问他们此刻从何处来。

他答道：“在东边的霍尔姆加德做客归来。我们整个夏天在比亚尔马兰和巴拉加德一带劫掠。收获并非空手。”

“等你从这里开船，”布伊说，“我看那批战利品得留在这儿了。”

西格瓦迪说道：“船都满员了，托基。咱们不如径直划出去：趁这些兔子还没受惊、翘起尾巴逃走，先把它们拿下。”

沃尔夫伯爵在船尾斯蒂尔比约恩身旁，凑他耳边说：“这是鲁莽的蠢行，我早说过。你没见他们正交头接耳商量对策么？这于我们决无好处。”

“由它去，”斯蒂尔比约恩说。他高声向帕尔纳托基喊道：“收下我们：你们会发觉，寻朋友寻到的并不是要吃掉你们和你们食物的饕餮之徒。你们正缺一个人，我就是你们的人。”

===Original===
“Thou art a boy,” shouted Palnatoki then. “Come back in a year or twain, with a beard grown. Then I’ll talk to thee.” But for all his scornful words, he stood looking still on Styrbiorn long and narrowly, as if taken by somewhat in the face of him, or in the carriage and gait of him, or in the voice of his speech. Styrbiorn’s brow blackened at his words.

Bui let out a great laugh, and cried aloud and said, “Home with thee to thy mammy, crow-chick. If I have not more hair of my tail than thou of thy face, let me end my days in a stinking swine-sty as dry-nurse to the new-weaned yelts.”

“There be hand-smiters here as well as tongue-smiters,” said Styrbiorn.

“And I have a switch,” cried Bui, “for to smite thy backside withal.”

Palnatoki abode yet looking on him from the wall, above the lazy wash and lap of the sea-swell. And now he shouted again to him on the ship and said, “I have told thee I am no lover of guests nor guestings. But my redes are reckoned wholesome. And this is my rede to thee, to put up your helm and get you away from Jomsburg whiles ye may.”

For answer, Styrbiorn let wear his ship yet closer for easier speech with them on the wall. And now might they have yet clearer sight of him, and of the great growth and strength that was his and the fair and likely seeming of the man, the while he spake with Palnatoki. And this was the end of their talking, that Styrbiorn said he was not minded to turn away from Jomsburg, but if they thought him over young to be had into their law and fellowship, then let them try it out, since by handy-stripes at equal odds is a man best known.
